REPLY BRIEF OF APPELLANT, 
THE OHIO MANUFACTURERS’ ASSOCIATION ENERGY GROUP 
 
 
I. 
INTRODUCTION 
Based on the speculative and duplicative load forecasts and the unsupported assertions 
presented by a monopoly utility, the Ohio Power Company (“AEP”), the Public Utilities 
Commission of Ohio (“PUCO”) issued an Opinion and Order (hereinafter, “Order”) on July 9, 
2025 that effectively denounced certain types of businesses and said they are not welcome in the 
State of Ohio, and that the State’s economic development is less important than a monopoly 
utility’s bottom line.  Not only is the PUCO’s decision to deliberately and unjustifiably 
discriminate against certain businesses unjust, unreasonable, and unlawful, it sets a dangerous 
precedent of permitting monopoly utilities to unlawfully target certain kinds of customers based 
on their industry type and energy usage rather than their electric load characteristics or the costs 
that they cause on or to the system. 
Approval of AEP’s unduly discriminatory data center tariff (“Schedule DCT”), which 
discriminates against one type of customer based solely on its business function and use of 
electricity, was unlawful, unreasonable, inconsistent with Ohio law, and unsupported by the record 
evidence presented in this case.  Contrary to assertions made by the PUCO, AEP, and the Office 
of the Ohio Consumers’ Counsel (“OCC”) (collectively, “Appellees”), the PUCO’s approval of 
 
2 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
Schedule DCT was not supported by a demonstration of actual and measurable differences in the 
provision of electric services to the customers being discriminated against or the cost to serve those 
customers.  Moreover, in addition to being unduly discriminatory and unreasonable on its face, 
Schedule DCT threatens to impede Ohio’s future economic development by sending a clear 
message to the business community—that the utility makes up the rules of the game, choosing 
who to provide electric service to and at what cost.  Therefore, as explained in the Ohio 
Manufacturers’ Association Energy Group’s (“OMAEG”) merit brief and more fully below, the 
PUCO erred by:  (1) unjustly, unreasonably, and unlawfully approving AEP’s discriminatory 
Schedule DCT in violation of R.C. 4905.32, R.C. 4905.33, and R.C. 4905.35, due process, and the 
Equal Protection Clause; (2) failing to find that AEP’s self-imposed moratorium was unlawful in 
violation of the Certified Territories Act (“CTA”) (R.C. 4933.83) and R.C. 4905.22 and failing to 
impose corresponding penalties; (3) finding that AEP demonstrated an actual transmission 
constraint that requires a solution without sufficient record evidence in violation of R.C. 4903.09; 
(4) finding that AEP’s Application was not an application for an increase in rates, thereby ignoring 
the plain language of R.C. 4909.18 and R.C. 4909.19; (5) failing to address and rule on the 
Administrative Law Judges’ (“ALJs”) unlawful decision to ignore statutory notice requirements 
and to commence the evidentiary hearing in violation of the plain language of R.C. 4909.18; and 
(6) upholding the ALJs’ rulings that prevented OMAEG from questioning AEP witnesses about 
matters directly relevant and material to this case in violation of R.C. 4903.09 and OMAEG’s due 
process rights. 
In response to the PUCO’s unreasonable and unlawful Order, OMAEG filed an Application 
for Rehearing on August 8, 2025 pursuant to R.C. 4903.10 (OMAEG Appx. 305) and Ohio 
Adm.Code 4901-1-35 (OMAEG Appx. 327), requesting that the PUCO reconsider its Order on 
 
3 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
the same grounds subject to this appeal.  OMAEG’s Application for Rehearing (August 8, 2025) 
(OMAEG Appx. 276–303).  The PUCO subsequently rejected OMAEG’s Application for 
Rehearing, and OMAEG appealed to this court.  Entry on Rehearing at ¶ 1 (September 3, 2025) 
(OMAEG Appx. 241); OMAEG’s Notice of Appeal (November 3, 2025) (OMAEG Appx. 1).  
OMAEG filed its merit brief on February 2, 2026.1  The PUCO filed its merit brief on March 24, 
2026,2 and intervening appellees AEP and OCC each filed their own briefs that same day.3 
The Appellees incorrectly argue that OMAEG misconstrues the applicable statutes and 
ignores relevant evidence.4  For the reasons set forth below, the plain meaning of the applicable 
statutes and the record evidence (or, in some cases, lack thereof) support OMAEG’s positions and 
contradict the arguments offered by the Appellees.  Accordingly, OMAEG seeks relief from this 
court and respectfully requests that the court reverse PUCO’s Orders (July 9, 2025 Order and 
September 3, 2025 Entry on Rehearing) as set forth herein in accordance with and in adherence to 
the plain language of applicable statutes. 
II. 
RESPONSIVE ARGUMENTS 
AEP bore the burden of proof in this case of demonstrating that the Stipulation and 
Recommendation filed on October 23, 2024 (“AEP Stipulation”) by AEP and a small group of 
other parties (collectively, “AEP Stipulation Signatories”) (1) was the product of serious 
bargaining between capable, knowledgeable parties; (2) as a package, is reasonable, serves the 
public interest, benefits customers; and (3) does not violate any important regulatory principles or 
practices.  Despite the improper limitations imposed by the ALJs, OMAEG and the majority of 
 
1 Merit Brief of Appellant The Ohio Manufacturers’ Association Energy Group (February 2, 2026) (“OMAEG Brief”). 
2 Merit Brief of Appellee The Public Utilities Commission of Ohio (March 24, 2026) (“PUCO Brief”). 
3 Merit Brief of Intervening Appellee Ohio Power Company (March 24, 2026) (“AEP Brief”); Merit Brief of 
Intervening Appellee the Office of the Ohio Consumers’ Counsel (March 24, 2026) (“OCC Brief”). 
4 See PUCO Brief; AEP Brief; OCC Brief. 
 
4 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
parties in this case (collectively, “Customer Coalition Stipulation Signatories”), all of whom 
signed the Stipulation and Recommendation filed on October 10, 2024 (“Customer Coalition 
Stipulation”) demonstrated that the AEP Stipulation did not satisfy the PUCO’s three-part test 
given that Schedule DCT constitutes undue and unreasonable “textbook discrimination,” and that 
AEP failed to demonstrate the existence of a transmission capacity constraint and need for the 
tariff.  Despite this, and contrary to the manifest weight of the evidence, the PUCO approved and 
adopted the AEP Stipulation and authorized AEP to file tariffs for Schedule DCT on July 9, 2025.  
See Order at ¶ 1 (OMAEG Appx. 142).  This approval was in error since AEP had not demonstrated 
a real transmission constraint that required a solution (i.e., need for AEP’s discriminatory tariff); 
or that the AEP Stipulation is not unduly discriminatory nor unlawful.  Order at ¶¶ 122, 128 
(OMAEG Appx. 215–16, 219).  Additionally, the PUCO’s decisions to uphold the ALJs’ improper 
rulings that:  (1) allowed the hearing to go forward in violation of R.C. 4909.18; and (2) limited 
cross-examination of AEP witnesses Ali and McKenzie in violation of due process and R.C. 
4909.09 were unjust, unreasonable, and unlawful as set forth herein and in OMAEG’s merit brief. 
PROPOSITION OF LAW NO. 1:  The PUCO unlawfully and unreasonably 
approved Schedule DCT as part of the AEP Stipulation, which is not based on 
actual, material differences in the provision of electric service and is therefore 
unduly discriminatory and unlawful. 
As recognized by all three Appellees, Ohio law prohibits utilities from drawing 
unreasonable and unjustified distinctions between similarly situated customers.  PUCO Brief at 7; 
AEP Brief at 11; OCC Brief at 11.  See also R.C. 4905.32; R.C. 4905.33; R.C. 4905.35.  Disparate 
treatment among customers is permitted only if there exists a reasonable basis for that differing 
treatment.  PUCO Brief at 7, citing Allnet Communications Serv., Inc. v. Pub. Util. Comm., 70 
Ohio St.3d 202, 207 (1994).  However, in this case, the AEP Stipulation Signatories failed to 
demonstrate the existence of such a reasonable basis.  Schedule DCT is unduly discriminatory and 
 
5 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
should not have been approved.  The PUCO is required to protect all customers from undue 
discrimination, but the PUCO Orders do just the opposite by authorizing AEP to target a particular 
type and group of customers based solely on their business function and end use of electricity. 
As recognized by AEP itself, “rate differentiation is permissible so long as it is grounded 
in legitimate, material differences in the service being provided or the circumstances under which 
it is provided.”  (Emphasis added) AEP Brief at 11.  Additionally, the party claiming that such 
material differences exist must support their claim with substantial, reliable, and probative 
evidence.  AEP Brief at 10.  See also PUCO Brief at 8–9; OCC Brief at 15.  Contrary to Appellees’ 
arguments otherwise, such evidence was lacking in this case.  The record is devoid of sufficient 
evidence demonstrating an actual and measurable difference between the electric service that AEP, 
as the distribution utility, renders to data centers and to other large load customers, and/or 
demonstrating an actual and measurable difference between the circumstances and conditions of 
AEP’s service to each set of customers. 
With respect to the service that AEP actually provides, Data Center Coalition (“DCC”) 
witness Higgins testified that AEP provides the same service (electric distribution service) to data 
center customers as it does to other, similarly-sized large load customers.  DCC Exhibit 9 at 15 
(Higgins November Testimony) (November 8, 2024) (Supp. Appx. 17).  Large load industrial 
customers, data center or otherwise, using the same capacity will require the same transmission 
and distribution infrastructure, the same equipment, the same voltage, the same electricity, and the 
same commitment to reliability from AEP.  With respect to the circumstances and conditions of 
that service, as explained in OMAEG’s merit brief, the AEP Stipulation Signatories offered no 
quantitative analysis (i.e., a cost of service study (“COSS”)) demonstrating a difference between 
AEP’s cost to serve data center customers versus its cost to serve non-data center, large load 
 
6 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
customers.  The AEP Stipulation Signatories similarly failed to offer any quantitative analysis (i.e., 
risk modeling) demonstrating a difference between the risk associated with serving data center 
customers versus non-data center, large load customers.  DCC Exhibit 9 at 15 (Higgins August 
Testimony) (August 29, 2024) (Supp. Appx. 97) (explaining that “the Company has not shown 
any actual or measurable difference between the service it provides to data center customers and 
the service it provides to other customers with equivalent or similar loads, and indeed, none 
exists—the Company provides substantially the same service to both”). 
In defending its unreasonable decision to approve Schedule DCT despite its unjustified 
discriminatory nature, the PUCO cited Migden-Ostrander v. Pub. Util. Comm., which Appellees 
similarly rely upon for the proposition that differences reasonably affecting the expense or 
difficulty of performing the same or similar service in different areas or circumstances may be 
reflected in cost recovery variations/differences.  Order at ¶¶ 129 (OMAEG Appx. 220–21); PUCO 
Brief at 8; AEP Brief at 11–12; OCC Brief at 11–12, all citing Migden-Ostrander v. Pub. Util. 
Comm., 102 Ohio St.3d 451, 459 (2004).  However, the reliance on Migden-Ostrander is 
misplaced.  Instead, the case supports rejecting Schedule DCT.  In Migden-Ostrander, this court 
upheld the PUCO’s approval of line extension tariffs that permitted different charges for different 
customers based on actual, demonstrated differences in the electrical requirements, such as voltage 
levels, load, and load factors, which directly affect the cost of line extensions, necessary for 
providing service to specific customers.  Migden-Ostrander v. Pub. Util. Comm., 102 Ohio St. 3d 
459. 
In contrast to Migden, there has been no cost justification presented in this case to 
demonstrate that serving data centers involves greater expense or difficulty compared to other large 
load customers.  This lack of cost justification is precisely why a cost-of-service study or other 
 
7 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
verifiable analysis is necessary if AEP wishes to justify its disparate treatment of a certain kind of 
business.  Similarly, while the Appellees all cite the purported “studies” conducted by AEP back 
in 2023, as OMAEG explained in its merit brief, a single page document (double-sided) 
summarizing studies that AEP ostensibly performed is not sufficient evidence to demonstrate that 
there is a reasonable basis for AEP’s discriminatory treatment against a particular type of customer, 
nor for the PUCO to authorize said discrimination.  OMAEG Brief at 11–13.  Lastly, while the 
PUCO presented a long list of alleged reasons why data center customers differ from other large 
load customers (a list that AEP echoed in its merit brief), the record demonstrated that those claims 
were either false, unsupported by the evidence, or not unique to data center customers but rather 
characteristics to all large loads.  Order at ¶¶ 129 (OMAEG Appx. 220–21); AEP Brief at 13–15. 
Therefore, and for all the reasons set forth above and in its merit brief, OMAEG 
respectfully requests that the court disregard the Appellees’ contrary arguments and find that the 
PUCO lacked sufficient record evidence upon which to base its finding that Schedule DCT is not 
unduly discriminatory.  As such, the PUCO Orders should be reversed.  
PROPOSITION OF LAW NO. 2:  The PUCO unlawfully and unreasonably found 
that AEP’s unauthorized, self-imposed moratorium was reasonable and 
consequently failed to impose appropriate sanctions and forfeitures on AEP. 
The PUCO’s decision to sanction AEP’s unprecedented, self-imposed, unilaterally 
enforced, and not approved by the PUCO, moratorium that has been in place since 2023 was unjust, 
unreasonable, and unlawful under the CTA (R.C. 4933.83) and R.C. 4905.22.  Moreover, sufficient 
rationale was not provided for the PUCO’s decision in violation of R.C. 4903.09.  As recognized 
by the Appellees, AEP has a statutory obligation to serve the customers in its service territory.  
PUCO Brief at 17–18; AEP Brief at 22; OCC Brief at 15–16.  Along with AEP’s exclusive right 
to provide electric service to all electric load centers located within its certified territory comes the 
obligation to furnish necessary and adequate service and facilities to meet the reasonable needs of 
 
8 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
their customers, and to ensure the availability to consumers of adequate, reliable, safe, efficient, 
nondiscriminatory, and reasonably priced retail electric service.  R.C. 4905.22; R.C. 4933.83; R.C. 
4928.02(A). 
Despite the plain language of these statutes establishing that AEP does not have the 
discretion to simply refuse to serve a type of customer on a whim, Appellees argue that AEP’s 
unauthorized moratorium and unilateral decision to cease connecting and providing service to a 
particular type of customer were somehow acceptable.  However, Appellees’ rationale fails here 
and cannot be used to justify AEP’s improper actions since, similar to Schedule DCT itself, AEP 
failed to present sufficient evidence that such a moratorium was in fact necessary.  While the 
PUCO may have created a new exception to the general rule about providing sufficient service, 
the utility seeking to invoke that exception should still be required to demonstrate that its current 
situation justifies applying that exception.  But AEP failed to do this.  For example, when asked in 
discovery to produce the studies that underpinned or justified AEP’s unprecedented decision to 
refuse to connect a certain type of customer, AEP produced only a two-page summary of three 
load scenarios that AEP’s transmission planning group performed in early 2023.  See Sidecat Ex. 
9 (Response to Staff-DR 2-2) (OMAEG Appx. 796); Sidecat Ex. 10 (Response to Staff DR 2-2 
Attachment 1) (OMAEG Appx. 797).  Moreover, AEP’s own long-term forecast reports (“LTFR”) 
do not reflect the unprecedented growth that AEP claims is nigh, with AEP’s 2023 LTFR showing 
negligible load growth over the next ten years, and AEP’s 2024 LTFR—which was filed one month 
before this case was initiated—included nothing close to the amount of customer load that AEP 
now claims will come on line.  RESA Ex. 2 (2023 LTFR) (OMAEG Appx. 857); OMAEG Ex. 35 
at 15 (Seryak August Testimony) (OMAEG Appx. 475). 
 
9 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
While the Appellees assert that the record contains sufficient evidence to justify AEP’s 
decision to impose the moratorium, they can cite to little more than AEP’s own assertions and 
testimony as “evidence” that AEP cannot reliably and adequately take on all new data center load 
within its service territory.  See PUCO Brief at 19–20; AEP Brief at 23–24; OCC Brief at 16.  
Appellees also ignore AEP’s other admissions on the record that it relied on speculative and 
duplicative load forecasts, has no way of separating committed data center customers from 
speculative ones, cannot speculate about what specific projects or costs would be involved in this 
transmission buildout, and had not conducted any specific analysis of potential transmission 
investment to be built in the future.  See Tr. Vol. I at 187–88, 269 (OMAEG Appx. 345–46, 368); 
Tr. Vol. II at 351, 364–72 (OMAEG Appx. 371, 384–92); OMAEG Ex. 22 (Response to OMAEG-
INT-01-041) (OMAEG Appx. 457); DCC Ex. 5 (OEG-INT-1-2) (OMAEG Appx. 867); OMAEG 
Ex. 23 (Response to DCC-INT-02-001) (OMAEG Appx. 459).  These foregoing admissions by 
AEP on the record also demonstrate the inaccuracy of AEP’s claim that OMAEG failed to offer 
contrary evidence in its initial brief.  AEP Brief at 27.  OMAEG presented a plethora of evidence—
much of it elicited from AEP itself—demonstrating that AEP lacked a rigorous quantitative 
analysis regarding key matters in this case, such as its purported need to force a certain type of 
customer into an industry-specific tariff, the specific projects or costs that would be involved in 
this transmission buildout, the potential transmission investment to be built in the future and who 
would build it, and the current amount of reserve capacity for additional load growth in Central 
Ohio.  Additionally, while the Appellees claim that AEP “proactively” initiated this case in order 
to create a solution to the alleged transmission capacity constraint, these assertions are undermined 
by the fact that AEP waited nearly two years between initiating its moratorium and filing its 
proposed tariffs. 
 
10 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
As a creature of statute, the PUCO must give full effect to the statutory language of R.C. 
4905.22, R.C. 4933.83, and R.C. 4928.02(A).  Penn Central Transportation Co. v. Pub. Util. 
Comm., 35 Ohio St.2d 97, 298 N.E.2d 97 (1973).  However, as explained in OMAEG’s merit brief, 
the PUCO showed little concern for AEP’s flagrant flouting of the law.  OMAEG Brief at 16–17.  
As a matter of law, the PUCO should not have condoned AEP making a unilateral, illegal, and 
factually unsupported decision to simply refuse to serve certain kinds of customers, nor should the 
PUCO have simply accepted AEP’s representations without further requiring additional, 
independent evidence to verify said claims.  Because the PUCO erroneously sectioned AEP’s 
moratorium after the fact, the PUCO Orders fail to impose penalties on AEP, which further signals 
to both AEP and other monopoly utilities that breaking the law, discriminating against customers, 
and actively choosing not to serve certain customers can and will go unpunished.  The PUCO could 
have and should have imposed penalties to prevent such illegal gamesmanship in the future, and 
the PUCO’s failure to do so was therefore unjust, unreasonable, and unlawful.  Therefore, the 
PUCO’s Order were unjust, unreasonable, and unlawful as they failed to find that AEP’s 
moratorium was unlawful under R.C. 4905.22, R.C. 4933.83, and R.C. 4928.02(A), and 
consequently failed to impose appropriate sanctions and forfeitures on AEP.  As such, the PUCO 
Orders should be reversed. 
PROPOSITION OF LAW NO. 3:  The PUCO unlawfully and unreasonably found 
that AEP demonstrated a real, pending transmission capacity constraint 
supported by substantial, competent evidence. 
In violation of R.C. 4903.09, the PUCO’s finding that AEP demonstrated an actual 
transmission constraint that required a remedy was not supported by sufficient record evidence, 
but rather by AEP’s own assertions and testimony underpinned by speculative load forecasts and 
a single page document summarizing studies that AEP ostensibly performed two years prior. 
 
11 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
Like the PUCO Orders themselves, the Appellees offer only AEP’s own claims and 
testimony to support AEP’s speculative assertions regarding the existence of a hypothetical 
transmission capacity constraint.  Appellees also completely ignore AEP’s contrary admissions 
regarding the existence of said constraint.  See PUCO Brief at 22–25; AEP Brief at 28–32; OCC 
Brief at 17–19.  However, as explained in OMAEG’s merit brief, this evidence is not sufficient to 
demonstrate the existence of a current transmission capacity constraint, especially considering 
AEP’s contrary admissions regarding the existence of said constraint—particularly the various 
admissions that AEP failed to perform rigorous quantitative analysis regarding key matters in this 
case.  See OMAEG Ex. 22 (Response to OMAEG-INT-01-041) (OMAEG Appx. 457); DCC Ex. 
5 (OEG-INT-1-2) (OMAEG Appx. 867); OMAEG Ex. 23 (Response to DCC-INT-02-001) 
(OMAEG Appx. 459); Tr. Vol. I at 262 (OMAEG Appx. 361); Tr. Vol. VII at 1504 (OMAEG 
Appx. 395).  Moreover, Staff witness Healey admitted during the hearing that Staff did not perform 
its own analysis to determine if a transmission constraint actually exists, meaning that AEP’s 
claims were not independently verified by an independent party, and the only quantitative evidence 
supporting AEP’s position was generated by AEP itself, or one of its affiliates.  Tr. Vol. XII at 
2667–69 (Supp. Appx. 156–59). 
AEP attempts to dismiss OMAEG’s concerns about the one-page summary document 
provided by the AEP Transmission Planning organization by mischaracterizing those concerns as 
a mere questioning of the format of the evidence that “has no bearing on the merits of the studies.”  
AEP Brief at 31.  However, it is not merely the format of this summary document—which both 
the PUCO and the Appellees so heavily rely on when concluding that AEP demonstrated the 
existence of an actual transmission capacity constraint—that OMAEG takes issue with.  As 
explained more fully in OMAEG’s merit brief, the fact that the studies were conducted by one of 
 
12 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
AEP’s own affiliates and never independently verified calls the veracity of the results/conclusion 
into question.  Moreover, since the document provides only a narrative summary of the studies 
conducted by the AEP Transmission Planning organization, the results and methodology could not 
be rigorously analyzed and/or verified by other experts.  While cross-examination on the exhibit 
was afforded to OMAEG and other parties, this is not a sufficient substitute to having the actual 
studies in hand.  OMAEG and the Customer Coalition Signatories—as well as the PUCO—had no 
way of knowing what information might have been omitted or glossed over in AEP’s summary.  
Consequently, OMAEG’s cross-examination was necessarily limited and may not have been as 
robust as it otherwise might have been.  AEP should not be allowed to indirectly dictate the scope 
of opposing parties’ cross though its continuous refusal to provide any actual transmission studies 
to support its claim that a transmission capacity constraint exists.  Such gamesmanship is both 
inappropriate and should not be rewarded, yet that is precisely what the PUCO did when it 
essentially chose to rely solely on AEP’s own representations (or that of its bias affiliate) regarding 
this integral matter.  Given the insufficiency of the evidence offered by AEP to support its 
transmission capacity constraint claim, the PUCO’s finding that AEP demonstrated the existence 
of a transmission capacity constraint violates R.C. 4903.09 because the finding cannot be 
supported by sufficient record evidence.  Therefore, for the reasons set forth above and in 
OMAEG’s merit brief, OMAEG respectfully requests that the Court deem the PUCO’s 
determination unlawful and against the manifest weight of the evidence, and reverse the PUCO 
Orders accordingly. 
PROPOSITION OF LAW NO. 4:  The PUCO unlawfully and unreasonably 
concluded that AEP Ohio’s application was not for an increase in rates. 
Based on the plain language of R.C. 4909.18 and the contents of AEP’s Application, the 
PUCO should have determined that AEP’s Application was an application for an increase in rates, 
 
13 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
and that the Application did not comply with statutory requirements.  As explained in OMAEG’s 
merit brief, AEP does not dispute that its Application in this case was filed pursuant to R.C. 
4909.18, and that the express purpose for its filing was to create two new customer classes and 
two new corresponding tariffs.  AEP Ex. 1 at 1 (Application) (OMAEG Appx. 329).  Moreover, 
Appellees agree with OMAEG that the critical question here is whether the proposal would raise 
rates for existing customers currently being served under AEP’s tariffs.  PUCO Brief at 26; AEP 
Brief at 35; OCC Brief at 21.  See also OMAEG Brief at 23–24.  As stated by OCC, if the approval 
of AEP’s Application would increase rates for existing customers, AEP would be required to file 
notice and abide by other filing requirements.  OCC Biref at 21. 
While the PUCO’s Order and Appellees contend that AEP’s Application is not for an 
increase in rates, but rather for the establishment of new rates that will not impact existing 
customers due to grandfathering provisions, this ignores the actual text of the Application.  See 
Order at ¶ 25 (OMAEG Appx. 148); PUCO Brief at 26; AEP Brief at 35; OCC Brief at 21.  
Specifically, while the Application does include a grandfathering provision for certain existing 
load, it increases rates or has the potential to increase rates for existing customers.  Additionally, 
that same paragraph explicitly states that “as part of its next base distribution rate case or some 
other appropriate proceeding in the future, the Company reserves the right to migrate those 
grandfathered customers to the new tariffs.”  AEP Ex. 1 at 1 (Application) (OMAEG Appx. 329).  
In other words, the rates of existing customers may be impacted by the approval of AEP’s 
Application.  And even if some existing customers are initially exempt, that exemption is not 
intended to last (and, indeed, in its most recent rate case, AEP attempted to do just that by 
eliminating the grandfathering provision of Schedule DCT).  Moreover, the Application 
specifically states that the proposed data center tariff will be appliable to existing customers who 
 
14 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
expand their existing load by  25 MW after the effective date of the tariff.  AEP Ex. 1 at 8 
(Application) (OMAEG Appx. 336).  Once again, AEP’s Application does not simply seek to 
establish new rates applicable to certain customers going forward, the Application seeks to 
increase the rates for some existing customers immediately and for others in the future.  This is the 
very definition, according to the Appellees themselves, of an application for an increase in rates. 
While OMAEG does not disagree that AEP’s Application proposed entirely new tariffs, 
the Application itself makes clear that these new tariffs can and will be applied to existing 
customers.  Consequently, AEP was seeking to impose new charges on customers currently being 
served under AEP’s existing tariffs.  Therefore, the Application should have been deemed an 
application for an increase in rates subject to additional procedural and filing requirements.  As 
such, the PUCO should have rejected AEP’s Application because it did not satisfy the requirements 
set forth in R.C. 4909.18 for applications for an increase in rates.  This rejection could have been 
accompanied by a directive for AEP to file a rate case if the Company wanted to create two new 
classifications of customers and establish new tariffs for a new type of service.  Had this been 
done, a proper COSS would have been performed, which would have provided much-needed 
insights into exactly how much it would cost AEP to serve a particular type of customer it claims 
is placing additional strain on AEP’s current system. 
For all the reasons set forth above and in its merit brief, OMAEG respectfully requests that 
this court find that the PUCO unjustly, unreasonably, and unlawfully failed to find that AEP’s 
Application was an application for an increase in rates that failed to meet the requirements set forth 
in R.C. 4909.18.  As such, the PUCO Orders should be reversed. 
 
15 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
PROPOSITION OF LAW NO. 5:  The PUCO unlawfully and unreasonably 
upheld the ALJs’ decision regarding statutory notice requirements under R.C. 
4909.18. 
In accordance with R.C. 4909.18, once this case was scheduled for hearing, the PUCO 
should have directed AEP to provide proper notice to the public of the evidentiary hearing.  The 
PUCO’s failure to do so was a clear and unambiguous violation of R.C. 4909.18, as was the ALJs’ 
subsequent refusal to stay the hearing until after the statutorily-mandated notice requirements set 
forth in R.C. 4909.18 were satisfied.  Tr. Vol. I at 37 (OMAEG Appx. 343). 
As discussed more fully in OMAEG’s merit brief, applications filed pursuant to R.C. 
4909.18 are either for an increase in rates or not.  For the reasons set forth above, contrary to the 
Appellees’ arguments that AEP’s Application was not for an increase in rates, the stated purpose 
of the Application and its contents demonstrate otherwise.  That said, even if the Application was 
not for an increase in rates, the statutory notice requirement still applies since the PUCO 
subsequently set the matter for hearing, thereby indicating that the PUCO believed AEP’s 
proposed tariffs appeared unjust or unreasonable.  R.C. 4909.18 (OMAEG Appx. 314).  While 
Appellees argue that the PUCO set this matter for hearing at its own discretion rather than due to 
the unjustness and unreasonableness of AEP’s proposed tariffs, such was never indicated by the 
PUCO prior to the issuance of its Entry on Rehearing.  This is in contrast to the cases cited by 
AEP, wherein matters were set for hearing, without notice, due to the existence of a prior 
stipulation (as explained by the PUCO itself in its entries setting those matters for hearing).  See 
AEP Brief at 43.  While it may be true that the PUCO possesses the discretion to set a matter for 
hearing on an application not for an increase in rates without making a determination that the 
application may be unjust or unreasonable, based on the information provided by the PUCO itself 
prior to the hearing, such did not appear to be the case here. 
 
16 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
As to the matter of whether substantial compliance existed as allowed under R.C. 4905.09, 
such substantial compliance did not occur in this case, which is readily distinguishable from the 
cases cited by AEP for several reasons.  See AEP Brief at 44; PUCO Brief at 28–29; OCC Brief at 
23.  First, the entries establishing the procedural schedules for the cases cited by AEP all included 
explicit notice directives.  In the Matter of the Regulation of the Purchased Gas Adjustment Clause 
Contained within the Rate Schedules of Columbia Gas of Ohio, Inc., PUCO Case No. 93-22-GA-
GCR, 1993 WL 13742105, Entry at ¶ 4 (February 25, 1993) (Supp. Appx. 162); In the Matter of 
the Complaint of Gary S. Bohn, PUCO Case No. 85-127-GE-CSS, 1985 WL 1171846, Entry at 1–
2 (February 20, 1985) (Supp. Appx. 166–67); In the Matter of the Request by the Ohio Dept. of 
Transp., PUCO Case No. 22-324-RR-RCP, Entry at ¶ 8 (January 20, 2023) (Supp. Appx. 169).  In 
contrast, none of the procedural entries issued in this case prior to the hearing date included a 
directive for AEP to provide notice of the evidentiary hearing.  As such, whereas in those other 
cases the PUCO’s subsequent directives to publish late notices could be construed as curing prior, 
inadvertent failures to provide notice as directed.  But, in this case, the PUCO failed to even direct 
AEP to issue notice, in violation of R.C. 4909.18.  Second, the hearings held in the cases cited by 
AEP were drastically different from the hearing in this case in scale, scope, and public interest.  
The hearing held in PUCO Case No. 93-22-GA-GCR involved an uncontested stipulation, unlike 
this case wherein two opposing stipulations were filed.  The hearing held in PUCO Case No. 85-
127-GE-CSS was for a customer complaint, which involved issues of far less import to the general 
public since it only affected the customer involved.  And the hearing held in PUCO Case No. 22-
324-RR-RCP was on a village seeking an exemption from a PUCO rule, which was far less 
contested than the AEP tariff case and had far less far-reaching effects.  Third, unlike the AEP 
tariff case, none of these other cases involved novel issues wherein the parties involved could have 
 
17 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
significantly benefitted from public input prior to the start of the hearing and cross-examination of 
the witnesses.  In short, the prior cases cited by AEP wherein the PUCO cured notice deficiencies 
are simply not comparable to the present case and are distinguishable given the important, 
precedential issue involved, the two contested stipulations, and the number of parties involved. 
Lastly, contrary to Appellees’ assertions, OMAEG was prejudiced by the PUCO’s failure 
to follow and enforce statutory notice requirements due to the lack of public input prior to the 
hearing.  As noted above, given the precedential and novel nature of this case, public input prior 
to the start of the hearing would have benefitted the parties involved and potentially aided in the 
crafting of cross-examination.  However, this was denied to OMAEG and other parties since notice 
and a public hearing were not provided until after the hearing was underway and certain witnesses, 
such as AEP witness Ali, had already taken the stand and been cross-examined, thus foreclosing 
parties’ ability to cross AEP’s witness further had new insights become available following the 
late public hearing. 
For the reasons set forth above and in OMAEG’s merit brief, the ALJs’ decision not to 
continue the hearing until after the statutory notice requirements were satisfied violated R.C. 
4909.18 and the due process rights of the parties, and the PUCO’s failure to address this issue in 
its Order and barebones “explanation” offered in its Entry on Rehearing violate R.C. 4903.09.  
Therefore, OMAEG respectfully requests that the court find that the PUCO’s decision to ignore 
statutory notice requirements violated statutory due process, R.C. 4909.18, and R.C. 4903.09 and 
reverse the PUCO Orders accordingly. 
PROPOSITION OF LAW NO. 6:  The PUCO unlawfully and unreasonably 
upheld the ALJs’ improper rulings limiting OMAEG’s cross-examination. 
As explained more fully in OMAEG’s merit brief, the PUCO erred by unjustly, 
unreasonably, and unlawfully upholding the ALJs’ rulings that improperly narrowed the scope of 
 
18 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
OMAEG’s cross and denied the PUCO a full review and examination of facts and matters key to 
the disposition of this case by preventing OMAEG from asking questions and fully developing the 
record regarding (1) whether AEP considered other solutions to mitigate the alleged transmission 
capacity constraint aside from a discriminatory tariff, and (2) whether AEP was even acting in 
good faith when it initiated this case.  While Appellees argue that OMAEG received due process 
in accordance with the PUCO’s accepted six requirements, all of them ignore the simple fact that 
OMAEG was not permitted to fully cross-examine the other parties’ witnesses.  See PUCO Brief 
at 30–31; AEP Brief at 48–49; OCC Brief at 24; Vectren Energy Delivery of Ohio, Inc. v. Pub. 
Util. Comm., 113 Ohio St.3d 180, 863 N.E.2d 599, 2006-Ohio-1386 at ¶ 53. 
While Appellees attempt to dismiss OMAEG’s cross of AEP witness Ali as merely 
repetitive and duplicative, the record demonstrates that the excluded questions, while certainly part 
of a related series, were each important to the case as they demonstrated which generation 
facilities, if any, were included in Mr. Ali’s transmission planning model and the underlying 
assumptions of the model.  See Tr. Vol. I at 215 (OMAEG Appx. 347).  Since the model itself was 
not made available for examination and review, OMAEG’s cross questions were the only means 
available by which to explore Mr. Ali’s methodologies, underlying assumptions, and inputs.  
Moreover, Mr. Ali’s inability to testify about the underlying assumptions of his own modeling, as 
well as what projects he actually factored into the modeling went directly to his credibility. 
As for OMAEG’s cross of AEP witness McKenzie, contrary to Appellees’ claims, those 
questions bore direct relevance on the underlying proceeding, particularly with regard to the fact 
that, assuming there even is a transmission capacity constraint, the issue is one of AEP’s own 
making.  Whether this is the case or not is both relevant to this proceeding and should have been 
considered by the PUCO when evaluating the reasonableness of AEP’s Stipulation, whether AEP’s 
 
19 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
Stipulation was in the best interest of customers, whether AEP’s Stipulation violated any 
regulatory principle, and, of course, which of the competing stipulations at issue was the most 
reasonable under the circumstances.  Gamesmanship in the form of creating a problem and then 
offering a solution that simultaneously allows for increased profits should not be allowed or 
encouraged, yet this is precisely what may have happened here.  OMAEG should have been 
permitted to further flesh out these issues on cross-examination so that additional facts could be 
added to the record for the PUCO’s review. 
For the reasons set forth above and in OMAEG’s merit brief, the ALJs’ rulings stymieing 
OMAEG’s cross and preventing OMAEG from fully developing the foregoing issues resulted in 
the effective exclusion of facts and evidence relevant and material to this case, thereby causing 
prejudice to OMAEG and other parties.  OMAEG therefore requests that this court find that the 
PUCO’s decision to uphold the ALJs’ rulings was unjust, unreasonable, and unlawful, and reverse 
the PUCO Orders accordingly. 
III. 
CONCLUSION 
On its face, Schedule DCT is discriminatory, and sufficient evidence was not presented to 
demonstrate that this discrimination was based on some measurable difference in the furnishing of 
electric services.  As such, Schedule DCT and the AEP Stipulation should have been rejected.  The 
PUCO’s failure to do so and decision to instead approve Schedule DCT was therefore unjust, 
unreasonable, unlawful, and against the manifest weight of the evidence in violation R.C. 4903.09.  
Moreover, as explained above and in OMAEG’s merit brief, the hearing itself should not have 
proceeded when it did due to various procedural failures.  Additionally, during the hearing itself, 
the ALJs further compounded the existing procedural failures by unreasonably limiting OMAEG’s 
cross-examination of AEP’s witnesses.  For these reasons, OMAEG respectfully requests that the 
 
20 
 
4914-0155-6123, v. 4 
court find that the PUCO’s July 9, 2025 Order and September 3, 2025 Entry on Rehearing are 
unjust, unreasonable, and unlawful and reverse the PUCO’s decisions accordingly. 
 
Respectfully submitted,

11

The purpose of my testimony in opposition is to address the Joint Stipulation and 
13 
Recommendation filed on October 23, 2024 by AEP Ohio, Staff of the Public Utilities 
14 
Commission of Ohio, Ohio Energy Group, Office of the Ohio Consumers’ Counsel, 
15 
Walmart Inc., and the Ohio Partners for Affordable Energy.  For ease of exposition, I will 
16

Q.

19 
STIPULATION AS IT RELATES TO A REASONABLE RESOLUTION OF THE 
20 
ISSUES IN THIS CASE. 
21 
A. 
I recommend that the Commission reject the October 23 Stipulation because it fails to 
22 
satisfy the Commission’s three-prong test for consideration of a stipulation.   In particular, 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 3 of 171
- 2 - 
 
the October 23 Stipulation is unduly discriminatory in that it singles out a specific business 
1 
activity, data centers, for tariff conditions that are more onerous than those imposed on 
2 
otherwise similarly-situated customers in other industries.  In so doing, the October 23 
3 
Stipulation violates important regulatory principles and practices and is inconsistent with 
4 
the public interest.   In addition, the excessive minimum demand charges proposed in the 
5 
October 23 Stipulation are inconsistent with the regulatory principle of aligning costs with 
6 
cost causation and therefore also not in the public interest.     
7 
My testimony primarily focuses on two of the three criteria that the Commission 
8 
considers when evaluating settlement agreements and explains why the October 23 
9 
Stipulation fails to satisfy those criteria. Specifically, my testimony explains why the 
10 
October 23 Stipulation (1) as a package is not in the public interest and fails to benefit 
11 
ratepayers, and (2) violates important regulatory principles and practices. 
12 
Q. 
AT A HIGH LEVEL, WHAT IS YOUR REACTION TO THE APPROACH THE 
13 
OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION TAKES IN AN ATTEMPT TO MANAGE THE 
14 
CHALLENGES ASSOCIATED WITH DATA CENTER LOAD GROWTH IN AEP 
15 
OHIO’S SERVICE TERRITORY?  
16 
A. 
In my initial direct testimony filed in this case, as well as in my testimony in support of the 
17 
October 10 Stipulation, I have supported the adoption of long-term contracts combined 
18 
with minimum demand charges and collateral requirements for new large loads to address 
19 
the concerns raised by AEP Ohio in its Application over the need to accommodate load 
20 
growth in a manner that avoids cost shifting if investments are made to serve new load that 
21 
does not fully materialize.  Unfortunately, the October 23 Stipulation fails to provide a 
22 
package that accomplishes this objective in a reasonable way.  
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 4 of 171
- 3 - 
 
As a fundamental matter, the Commission can address data center load growth, and avoid 
1 
burdening manufacturers or other large energy users with more onerous terms, without 
2 
creating an unprecedented, discriminatory tariff as proposed in the October 23 Stipulation.  
3 
In advancing a discriminatory rate proposal, the October 23 Stipulation fails to consider 
4 
the following points:  
5 
o Data centers generally have larger loads than manufacturing customers, and so it is 
6 
possible to capture the vast majority of new data centers in a new tariff without 
7 
capturing the vast majority of new manufacturing customers by adopting a higher 
8 
threshold customer size as an applicability criterion.  
9 
o To the extent a higher size threshold still captures some new non-data center 
10 
customers, such a result is reasonable from the perspective of AEP Ohio’s 
11 
ratepayers, because those new customers require the same service obligations as 
12 
data centers.  
13 
o AEP Ohio’s Indiana and West Virginia affiliates are addressing similar load growth 
14 
challenges as AEP Ohio, but through size-based non-discriminatory tariff 
15 
proposals.  
16 
By proposing a tariff that puts a single business activity at a disadvantage relative to other 
17 
similarly-situated industrial customers, the October 23 Stipulation violates the important 
18 
regulatory principle proscribing unduly discriminatory service.   The Commission should 
19 
not allow this fundamental regulatory principle to be eroded because of the October 23 
20 
Stipulation’s preferences for one type of business activity over another. 
21 
Further, unlike the October 10 Stipulation, the October 23 Stipulation is not 
22 
anchored in an analysis of the incremental costs to serve data center customers.   Instead, 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 5 of 171
- 4 - 
 
it implicitly assumes that if a data center customer has a minimum billing demand less than 
1 
100%, a cost shift is created that forces non-data customers to bear the costs of 
2 
underutilized transmission capacity investment.1  But as my original testimony opposing 
3 
AEP Ohio’s Application shows, even with a 60% minimum demand charge and an eight 
4 
year term, new large loads would comfortably cover the costs of the incremental 
5 
transmission investments they cause, and exert downward pressure on other customers’ 
6 
rates.  Consequently, the minimum demand charge proposals in both the October 10 and 
7 
October 23 stipulation are higher than they need to be to ensure that AEP Ohio will recover 
8

9 
new transmission.  
10 
Finally, the October 23 Stipulation includes a new process for signing up data 
11 
center customers, ostensibly to create more predictability for those customers. But it is my 
12

would hinder, not facilitate, the contracting process.

III. 
THREE-PRONG TEST FOR CONSIDERATION OF A STIPULATION 
15 
Q. 
WHAT IS YOUR UNDERSTANDING OF THE STANDARD THE COMMISSION 
16 
APPLIES WHEN EVALUATING STIPULATIONS? 
17 
A. 
Although I am not an attorney, my understanding is the Commission traditionally evaluates 
18 
stipulations among two or more parties by applying a three-prong test. The prongs of that 
19 
test, as I understand them, are: 
20 
(1) Is the Stipulation a product of serious bargaining among capable, 
21 
knowledgeable parties? 
22 
                                                 
1 See, for example, Testimony in Opposition of Matthew McKenzie p. 22, lines 3-15.  
Supp. Appx. Page Page 6 of 171
- 5 - 
 
(2) Does the Stipulation, as a package, benefit ratepayers and the public interest? 
1 
(3) Does the Stipulation violate any important regulatory principle or practice? 
2 
Q. 
DO YOU BELIEVE THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION IS THE PRODUCT OF 
3 
SERIOUS BARGAINING AMONG CAPABLE AND KNOWLEDGEABLE 
4 
PARTIES? 
5 
A. 
I have no reason to doubt that the parties to the October 23 Stipulation are capable and 
6 
knowledgeable.  Importantly, however, the parties to the October 23 Stipulation—which 
7 
represent a minority of parties to the case—do not include any data center customers, which 
8 
exclusively are the entities that would be subject to the Data Center Tariff being proposed 
9 
by the October 23 Stipulation.  So while the October 23 Stipulation may be a product of 
10 
bargaining among certain signatories, the signatories do not include the customers most 
11 
directly affected by the tariff proposal, which is a glaring deficiency in that stipulation.  
12 
Q. 
REGARDING THE SECOND CRITERION, DOES THE OCTOBER 23 
13 
STIPULATION, AS A PACKAGE, BENEFIT CUSTOMERS AND THE PUBLIC 
14 
INTEREST? 
15 
A. 
No. The October 23 Stipulation does not benefit customers and the public interest as a 
16 
package because it suffers from a number of fatal defects.  I will address each of these 
17 
defects in more detail below.  
18 
A.  The October 23 Stipulation’s Proposal to Discriminate Against Customers 
19 
Based on Business Activity Is Not in the Public Interest and Should Be 
20 
Rejected by the Commission.  
 
21 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 7 of 171
- 6 - 
 
Q. 
PLEASE DESCRIBE THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION’S APPLICABILITY 
1 
PROVISION. 
2 
A. 
The October 23 Stipulation proposes a new Data Center Tariff, termed Schedule DCT, 
3 
which would apply only to data centers and mobile data centers for new loads (or expansion 
4 
of existing loads) greater than 25 MW in the aggregate, where “aggregate” refers to all new 
5 
load of affiliated companies and companies with common ownership, as well as load at a 
6 
single location.  Customers subject to the Data Center Tariff could only be served under 
7 
that tariff.  Loads above 25 MW that have already signed a Letter of Agreement (“LOA”) 
8 
or Electric Service Agreement (“ESA”) by the effective date of the tariff would be 
9 
grandfathered, so long as the load does not expand by more than 25 MW above contracted 
10 
capacity under the existing ESA following the effective date of Schedule DCT.   
11 
Q. 
HOW 
DOES 
THE 
OCTOBER 
23 
STIPULATION’S 
APPLICABILITY 
12 
PROVISION DIFFER FROM THAT OF THE OCTOBER 10 STIPULATION? 
13 
A. 
The most fundamental difference is that the proposed tariff change in the October 23 
14 
Stipulation would be applied to a single business activity, data centers, whereas the October 
15 
10 Stipulation tariff change applies to any new large customer meeting the specified size 
16 
characteristics on a non-discriminatory basis.   The October 23 Stipulation also applies at 
17 
a smaller threshold, 25 MW versus 50 MW, and would apply throughout AEP Ohio’s 
18 
service territory, not just locations that are experiencing transmission capacity constraints, 
19 
which is the case for the October 10 Stipulation.     
 
20 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 8 of 171
- 7 - 
 
Q. 
IS THE PROPOSAL FOR A TARIFF THAT IS SPECIFIC TO DATA CENTERS 
1 
REASONABLE? 
2 
A. 
No.  The applicability provision in the October 23 Stipulation is unduly discriminatory.  
3 
The fundamental concerns raised by AEP Ohio in its Application center around the impacts 
4 
of substantial load growth by very large customers, and not around any impacts specific to 
5 
data center customers.   
6 
Q. 
PLEASE ELABORATE. 
7 
A. 
AEP Ohio’s fundamental reason for proposing a tariff change in this proceeding is to 
8 
mitigate the risk of its retail customers bearing the costs of transmission buildout—which 
9 
could include extra high-voltage transmission lines—required to serve large new loads, in 
10 
the event those loads do not materialize or shut down prematurely. In other words, the only 
11 
characteristic of data center customers driving the Company’s proposal is the size of those 
12 
customers’ loads. Any other type of customer with loads of a similar magnitude would 
13 
require similar transmission infrastructure if that customer were to seek interconnection in 
14 
a transmission constrained area; therefore, any new tariff designed to mitigate the risks 
15 
associated with new large loads should be applied on a non-discriminatory basis.  As a 
16 
practical matter, both stipulations will apply to new data center loads in transmission 
17 
constrained areas, but the October 10 Stipulation accomplishes this result without resorting 
18 
to a tariff that discriminates based on business activity or imposes onerous terms on 
19 
customers interconnecting in unconstrained areas where substantial incremental 
20 
transmission investment, such as new extra high-voltage transmission lines, would not be 
21 
required to serve them.  Moreover, the larger applicability threshold (50 MW versus 25 
22 
MW) in the October 10 Stipulation has the practical effect of excluding new manufacturing 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 9 of 171
- 8 - 
 
activity in that size range.  Thus, it has the virtue of addressing head-on the problem that 
1 
needs to be solved as identified by AEP Ohio in its Application, but does so without the 
2 
unreasonably discriminatory features of the October 23 Stipulation.    
3 
Q. 
WHY IS A TARIFF THAT IS SPECIFIC TO A PARTICULAR BUSINESS 
4 
ACTIVITY AS PROPOSED IN THE OCTOBER 23 SETTLEMENT UNDULY 
5 
DISCRIMINATORY? 
6 
A. 
As a first principle, rate classes should be established based on load characteristics rather 
7 
than the business in which the customer is engaged, as load characteristics are the 
8 
fundamental elements for determining cost causation.  The costs caused by a rate class are 
9 
driven by its aggregate load shape, the volume of power it consumes, and the number of 
10 
customers served, not the end-use per se that its power consumption supports.  If it is 
11 
determined that new large loads require special terms to ensure long-term capacity 
12 
commitments that mitigate the risk of stranded transmission costs then such terms should 
13 
apply equally to all new large loads of similar size, because those loads would implicate 
14 
the very same risk.  
15 
Q. 
DOES AEP OHIO ALREADY HAVE TARIFFS THAT ARE SPECIFIC TO 
16 
PARTICULAR BUSINESS OR PUBLIC SECTOR ENTITIES? 
17 
A. 
Yes, AEP Ohio has a handful of such tariffs today and it is worthwhile to take a closer look 
18 
at them to understand why none of them set a relevant precedent for the proposed Data 
19 
Center Tariff.      
20 
Q. 
PLEASE PROCEED. 
21 
A. 
One of the more common end-use or sector-specific tariffs in the electric industry, 
22 
including AEP Ohio, is for lighting, both area lighting and street lighting.  It is natural for 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 10 of 171
- 9 - 
 
lighting rate schedules to be tied to specific end-uses both because lighting is associated 
1 
with a specific load characteristic, i.e., night-time usage, and because these rate schedules 
2 
typically recover the costs of specific end-use equipment.   For example, AEP Ohio’s Area 
3 
Lighting rate schedule has 26 different rates that apply to unique pieces of equipment 
4 
owned by the Company and used by the customer.2  Similarly, AEP Ohio’s Street Lighting 
5 
rate schedule has 28 different rates that apply to unique pieces of equipment owned by the 
6 
Company and used by the customer.3   
7 
 
 
There is no analogue between lighting tariffs and the proposed Data Center Tariff: 
8 
the end-use equipment used by data centers is owned by the data center, not the utility.  
9 
Therefore, there is no reason to adopt a data center tariff to recover the costs of utility-
10 
owned end use equipment.  The existence of Area Lighting and Street Lighting tariffs does 
11 
not in any way set a relevant precedent for establishing a data-center-specific tariff. 
12 
Q. 
DOES AEP OHIO HAVE OTHER TARIFFS THAT ARE SPECIFIC TO 
13 
PARTICULAR BUSINESS OR PUBLIC SECTOR ACTIVITIES? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  AEP Ohio has School Service tariff.4  Notably, unlike the proposed Data Center 
15 
Tariff, the School Service tariff is not mandatory for applicable loads, and also unlike the 
16 
Data Center Tariff, it provides an advantage to customers on this tariff compared to the 
17 
otherwise applicable tariff.  Specifically, the School Service tariff provides an energy-only 
18 
rate that reduces the cost to lower-load-factor customers on this rate schedule compared to 
19 
standard General Service-Secondary or Primary rates.  Significantly, AEP Ohio is in the 
20 
                                                 
2 See Exhibit KCH-1-O. 
3 See Exhibit KCH-2-O. 
4 See Exhibit KCH-3-O. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 11 of 171
- 10 - 
 
process of eliminating the School Service tariff.  It is not available to any customers that 
1 
are not already taking service under this tariff. 
2 
 
 
Similarly, AEP Ohio has an Optional Church and School Service supplement that 
3 
is in the process of elimination and is not available to any customers that are not already 
4 
taking service under this tariff.5  Notably, this specialty supplement is tied to the customers’ 
5 
load characteristics in that it is provided to customers whose maximum demand normally 
6 
occurs in the off-peak period.  And unlike the proposed Data Center Tariff, the Optional 
7 
Church and School Service tariff provides a discount relative to the otherwise applicable 
8 
rate, specifically one-half of the metered demand for the transmission component of the 
9 
monthly bill.       
10 
 
 
Finally, AEP Ohio has a tariff specific to County and Independent Fairs,6 which 
11 
also have unique load characteristics in that the annual usage of such customers is sporadic.  
12 
The energy-only rate design for this tariff allows the occasional users of the electric system 
13 
on this tariff to avoid the General Service demand charge and the 60% minimum demand 
14 
requirement that would otherwise apply.  Thus, it is an advantageous rate relative to 
15 
standard service.  It is also tied to the class’s load characteristics (i.e., occasional usage).    
16 
 
 
In conclusion, none of the handful of AEP Ohio tariffs that appear to be specific to 
17 
particular activities provides a relevant precedent for adopting a data-center-specific tariff. 
18 
The proposed Data Center Tariff is mandatory and places data centers at a disadvantage 
19 
relative to other new large loads of similar size.   In contrast, the School Service tariff, the 
20 
Optional Church and School supplement, and the County and Independent Fair tariffs are 
21 
optional and provide customers on these schedules lower rates compared to the otherwise 
22 
                                                 
5 See Exhibit KCH-4-O. 
6 See Exhibit KCH-5-O. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 12 of 171
- 11 - 
 
applicable tariffs.  No customer receiving such an advantage could be construed to suffer 
1 
from undue discrimination.  Moreover, two of these specialty tariffs are slated for 
2 
elimination.  Clearly, the data-center-specific tariff proposed in the October 23 Stipulation 
3 
is a major step in the opposite direction of AEP Ohio’s general movement away from 
4 
sector-specific rates.  And it is entirely unnecessary.      
5 
Q. 
IN HIS TESTIMONY IN SUPPORT OF THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION, AEP 
6 
OHIO WITNESS MCKENZIE LISTS ELECTRIC VEHICLE TARIFFS, NET 
7 
METERING 
TARIFFS, 
GENERATION 
STATION 
POWER, 
AND 
A 
8 
COGENERATION AND SMALL POWER TARIFF AS EXAMPLES OF TARIFF 
9 
SCHEDULES THAT DRAW REASONABLE DISTINCTIONS BETWEEN 
10 
CUSTOMER CLASSES.7   DO YOU WISH TO COMMENT ON THIS POINT? 
11 
A. 
I agree that these tariff schedules make reasonable distinctions between customer classes 
12 
based on the characteristics of the service being provided. But none of these tariffs provide 
13 
a legitimate precedent that justifies adoption of a discriminatory tariff that is 
14 
disadvantageous to a customer engaged in a particular business activity.   
15 
 
 
Electric vehicle tariffs encourage customers to charge electric vehicles off-peak, 
16 
which is a usage characteristic related to cost of service; indeed Schedule RS-PEV provides 
17 
a credit to residential customers with separately metered electric vehicle chargers who 
18 
charge off-peak.   Net metering tariffs deal with the complex rate design issues associated 
19 
with customers that have behind-the-meter generation and may deliver power onto the grid.  
20 
The same is true with generation station power and cogeneration and small power 
21 
                                                 
7 Supplemental Testimony of Matthew S. McKenzie in Support of the AEP Ohio Stipulation, p. 36. See Exhibit 
KCH-6-O (RS-PEV tariff); Exhibit KCH-7-O (NEMS tariff); Exhibit KCH-8-O (GSP tariff); Exhibit KCH-9-O 
(COGEN tariff). 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 13 of 171
- 12 - 
 
production tariffs.  In fact, the Generation Station Power schedule specifically states that 
1 
customers on that schedule will be billed in accordance with the General Service schedule, 
2 
except for the special terms dealing with the delivery of power to the grid and metering.  
3 
The distinctions among these tariffs are valid because of their specific relationships to cost 
4 
causation and the unique natures of their service.  They do not represent examples of 
5 
applying disadvantageous terms to a set of customers that have a substantially similar usage 
6 
pattern as other customers, but are engaged in different business activity. 
7 
Q. 
HAS 
THE 
COMPANY 
SHOWN 
ANY 
ACTUAL 
OR 
MEASURABLE 
8 
DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THE SERVICE IT PROVIDES DATA CENTER 
9 
CUSTOMERS AND ANY OTHER CUSTOMERS WITH EQUIVALENT OR 
10 
SIMILAR LOADS? 
11 
A. 
No, the Company has not shown any actual or measurable difference between the service 
12 
it provides to data center customers and the service it provides to other customers with 
13 
equivalent or similar loads, and indeed, none exists—the Company provides substantially 
14 
the same service to both.  In his testimony in support of the October 23 Stipulation, witness 
15 
McKenzie argues that Schedule DCT should apply only to data centers because data centers 
16 
are triggering rapid growth, exhibit concentrated demand in a single location, and represent 
17 
concentrated risk in a single industry.8  I agree that the rapid growth and large size of data 
18 
centers justify taking action to ensure that new large loads, including  data centers, are 
19 
required to enter long-term contracts with minimum demand charges that are backed up by 
20 
collateral or other substantial security.  These are the essential ingredients for addressing 
21 
the concerns raised by AEP Ohio.  The October 10 Stipulation fully incorporates these 
22 
                                                 
8 Supplemental Testimony of Matthew S. McKenzie in Support of the AEP Ohio Stipulation, pp. 37-39. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 14 of 171
- 13 - 
 
features.  New data centers in transmission constrained locations will be subject to stringent 
1 
new terms that fully mitigate the risks identified by witness McKenzie.  But importantly, 
2 
the October 10 Stipulation accomplishes this objective without resorting to a 
3 
discriminatory tariff.    
4 
 
 
Data centers are large customers taking service at high voltage and should be 
5 
grouped with other customers that are similarly situated, such as manufacturers and other 
6 
large loads that have the same infrastructure or service requirements. The Commission 
7 
should reject the attempt in the October 23 Stipulation to apply the newly-proposed tariff 
8 
specifically to data centers.  Such an adverse discriminatory tariff does not have a relevant 
9 
precedent among the Company’s current tariffs and is not in the public interest.  Any 
10 
special tariff terms the Commission adopts to mitigate the risks of new large loads should 
11 
apply on a non-discriminatory basis to all such loads, irrespective of the customers’ 
12 
business activities. The Commission should refrain from singling out specific industries 
13 
for tariff conditions that are more onerous than those imposed on otherwise similarly-
14 
situated customers in other industries.   
15 
Q. 
HAS ANY AFFILIATE OF AEP OHIO FILED AN APPLICATION TO MODIFY 
16 
CONTRACT TERMS FOR LARGE CUSTOMERS TO ADDRESS ANTICIPATED 
17 
LOAD GROWTH WITHOUT RESORTING TO A DISCRIMINATORY TARIFF? 
18 
A. 
Yes.   On July 19, 2024, Indiana Michigan Power Company (“I&M”), an affiliate of AEP 
19 
Ohio, filed a petition to make changes to its Industrial Power Tariff in Indiana for large 
20 
load customers whose contract capacity exceeds 150 MW or is reasonably expected to 
21 
grow to exceed 150 MW at one or more aggregated premises.9   Like AEP Ohio, I&M is 
22 
                                                 
9 See Exhibit KCH-10-O. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 15 of 171
- 14 - 
 
proposing changes to its contract term and minimum demand charges in anticipation of 
1 
increased demand from data centers.   But unlike AEP Ohio and the October 23 Stipulation, 
2 
I&M designed its tariff changes in a manner that is not discriminatory to any particular 
3 
business activity.  While I do not agree with all elements in I&M’s proposal, its recent 
4 
filing provides a clear demonstration that it is unnecessary to respond to load growth 
5 
concerns by imposing a discriminatory tariff.    
6 
 
 
Similarly, on July 18, 2024, Appalachian Power Company and Wheeling Power 
7 
Company (“APCo”), another affiliate of AEP Ohio, filed an application to modify its 
8 
Schedules LCP and IP to enable APCo to negotiate contract terms with potential new 
9 
customers with loads in excess of 200 MW.10   In its application, APCo cited a large amount 
10 
of interest in new service from large load customers.  And while I do not agree with all of 
11 
the components in APCo’s proposal, its filing also demonstrates that addressing the 
12 
challenges of serving new large loads can be accomplished without resorting to a 
13 
discriminatory tariff, such as AEP Ohio proposed in its initial Application, and which the 
14 
October 23 Stipulation substantially incorporates. 
15 
B.  
The Minimum Demand Charge Proposed in the October 23 Stipulation for 
16 
Loads Greater than 62 MW Is Excessive and Should Be Rejected by the 
17 
Commission. 
18 
Q. 
PLEASE DESCRIBE THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION’S PROVISIONS 
19 
CONCERNING THE MINIMUM DEMAND CHARGE. 
20 
A. 
The minimum demand charge proposed in the October 23 Stipulation is a function of load 
21 
size, with the minimum demand charge increasing as the size of the customer’s load 
22 
                                                 
10 See Exhibit KCH-11-O. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 16 of 171
- 15 - 
 
increases.   As presented in Exhibit B of that stipulation, at 25 MW the minimum demand 
1 
charge is 60%, at 63 MW it is 75.08%, and at 117 MW and above, it is 85%.   
2 
Q. 
WHY IS THE MINIMUM DEMAND CHARGE IN THE OCTOBER 23 
3 
STIPULATION EXCESSIVE? 
4 
A. 
In my prior testimony submitted in this proceeding, I demonstrated that the combination 
5 
of an eight-year contract commitment, a 60% minimum demand charge provision, and 
6 
collateral/security requirements—would ensure that the Company receives sufficient 
7 
revenues from new large customers such that the Company comfortably recovers the 
8 
incremental transmission costs it incurs in order to serve those customers, even if those 
9 
customers’ anticipated loads do not fully materialize. 
10 
 
 
This assurance of cost recovery is due to the relationship between the incremental 
11 
cost of new transmission and the embedded cost of AEP Ohio’s current transmission as 
12 
recovered through the Base Transmission Cost Rider (“BTCR”), which is the ratemaking 
13 
mechanism through which AEP Ohio recovers its annual transmission revenue requirement 
14 
from its retail customers.11  Generally, the BTCR transmission revenue requirement is 
15 
updated annually, as is the allocation of BTCR costs to the retail customer classes.  Notably, 
16 
the BTCR charge as applied to large load customers is the main rate component affected 
17 
by the minimum demand charge proposals at issue in this case.  
18 
 
 
While it is true that the incremental cost of new transmission is more expensive 
19 
than the embedded cost of existing transmission of the same voltage, the type of new 
20 
transmission that would be built to accommodate a large expansion of AEP Ohio’s system 
21 
matters when it comes to determining whether the incremental cost of the new transmission 
22 
                                                 
11 See Exhibit KCH-12-O. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 17 of 171
- 16 - 
 
is greater or less than the average cost of the current system as recovered through the 
1 
BTCR.   
2 
 
 
For example, a new 765-kV transmission line, which AEP Ohio suggests may be 
3 
necessary to accommodate significant load growth, costs roughly 2.7 times the cost of a 
4 
138-kV line on a cost-per-mile basis, but can carry nearly 17 times the load.  On a dollars-
5 
per-MW basis, it is highly efficient transmission.12  At a cost of $6 million per mile, a 120-
6 
mile 765-kV line would have an incremental revenue requirement of around $1.45 per kW-
7 
month,13 a fraction of AEP Ohio’s current BTCR rate for transmission voltage customers 
8 
of $7.45 per kW month, and an even smaller fraction of AEP Ohio’s 1 CP BTCR rate of 
9 
$12.24 per kW-month – even before taking into account PJM cost sharing that would occur 
10 
with a Regional Transmission Facility of this voltage.  Even if the incremental cost of new 
11 
transmission were three times the $1.45 per kW-month estimate I am using here, it would 
12 
still be less than 60% of AEP Ohio’s current BTCR rate for transmission voltage, and 
13 
consequently materially lower than 75% of AEP Ohio’s current BTCR rate for 
14 
transmission voltage, which is representative of the lower range of the minimum demand 
15 
charges in the October 10 Stipulation.14 
16 
 
 
The key takeaway here is that new large loads will be charged AEP Ohio’s 
17 
(relatively expensive) BTCR rate and therefore make a material contribution to the 
18 
recovery of the Company’s current embedded costs, thereby putting downward pressure 
19 
on the BTCR rates for other customers, while causing incremental transmission costs that 
20 
under any reasonable scenario will be lower than the Company’s average embedded BTCR 
21 
                                                 
12 See Exhibit KCH-1, page 3, attached to my Direct Testimony. 
13 Based on a first-year revenue requirement of $111 million accommodating 6,407 MW of incremental load.  
14 That is, 3 x $1.45 = $4.35, whereas 60% x $7.45 = $4.47 and 75% x $7.45 = $5.59. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 18 of 171
- 17 - 
 
rates.   For these reasons, it is not necessary to increase the current 60% minimum demand 
1 
charge to ensure that new large loads recover the incremental cost of new transmission that 
2 
will be built to serve them.   While the 85% minimum demand charge proposed in the 
3 
October 23 Stipulation is an improvement over the 90% minimum demand charge 
4 
proposed by AEP Ohio in its Application, it is still excessive, and therefore not in the public 
5 
interest. 
6 
 
 
In contrast, the October 10 Stipulation offers three options for minimum demand 
7 
charges ranging from 85% down to 75%, with the lower minimums being available in 
8 
exchange for longer contract terms, which is an eminently reasonable tradeoff.    
9 
Q. 
IN HIS TESTIMONY IN OPPOSITION TO THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION, 
10 
AEP WITNESS MCKENZIE ARGUES THAT THE 75% MINIMUM DEMAND 
11 
CHARGE WOULD PROVIDE AN INCENTIVE FOR DATA CENTER 
12 
CUSTOMERS TO SIGN ESAS FOR MORE POWER THAN THEY NEED.15   
13 
WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE TO THIS ARGUMENT? 
14 
A. 
There is a fundamental inconsistency in witness McKenzie’s line of argument.  On the one 
15 
hand, AEP Ohio asserts that Schedule DCT is needed in the first instance to address the 
16 
uncertainties associated with future data center load, namely questions concerning whether 
17 
the load will materialize, and if it does, will it remain.  And in my own testimony, I have 
18 
not disputed the need to address these questions.  But after establishing the need to address 
19 
this uncertainty, witness McKenzie goes on to postulate that data center customers will 
20 
know exactly how much load they will require seven to ten years hence, and will use that 
21 
perfect foresight to deliberately oversubscribe their requirements, even though doing so 
22 
                                                 
15 Supplemental Testimony of Matthew S. McKenzie in Opposition to the Data Center Stipulation, pp. 22-23. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 19 of 171
- 18 - 
 
will subject them to the risk of higher minimum demand charges and load ramps if their 
1 
facility development does not proceed as initially planned.  In short, AEP Ohio uses 
2 
uncertainty to justify the need for Schedule DCT, but assumes perfect foresight on the part 
3 
of data centers to justify an 85% minimum demand charge. 
4 
 
 
Witness McKenzie also conflates his concerns about oversubscription with the 
5 
subject of cost recovery.  At AEP Ohio’s relatively high BTCR rates, a 75% minimum 
6 
demand charge over period of 9 to 16 years pursuant to Term C of the October 23 will not 
7 
leave other customers “holding the bag” for under-utilized capacity,16 as I have already 
8 
demonstrated.  
9 
C.  The Load Ramp Proposed in the October 23 Stipulation Does Not Provide 
10 
Sufficient Flexibility and Should Be Rejected by the Commission.

12

13 
A.

14

19
