20 
• 30% of Contract Capacity in Year 1 
21 
• 50% of Contract Capacity in Year 2 
22 
                                                 
16 Id., p. 22. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 20 of 171
- 19 - 
 
• 70% of Contract Capacity in Year 3 
1 
• 90% of Contract Capacity in Year 4 
2 
Q. 
WHAT IS YOUR ASSESSMENT OF THE LOAD RAMP PROPOSED IN THE 
3 
OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION? 
4 
A. 
Load ramp is an area in which the parties have been able to narrow their differences as a 
5 
result of the settlement process.   The difference between the stipulations concerns the first 
6 
three years of the load ramp period.  The October 10 Stipulation provides greater ramping 
7 
flexibility that also accommodates ramping at a steady rate, as reflected in the required 
8 
annual increases of 20% of Contract Capacity between Years 1 and 2, Years 2 and 3, and 
9 
Years 3 and 4.  Importantly, assurance of sufficient revenue recovery from the large load 
10 
customer is provided by the term requirements that are added to the end of the load ramp 
11 
period.  The additional contract years added to the load ramp period is a feature of both 
12 
stipulations that was not an element of AEP Ohio’s original Application.  This lengthening 
13 
of the contract term fully addresses any concerns that the lower capacity requirements 
14 
during the load ramp period could lead to an under-recovery of incremental transmission 
15 
costs during the term of the contract.   Overall, the October 10 Stipulation provides more 
16 
reasonable ramping metrics and better serves the public interest. 
17 
 D.  The Termination and Assignment Provision in the October 23 Stipulation Is 
18 
Inefficient. 
19 
Q. 
PLEASE DESCRIBE THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION’S PROVISIONS 
20 
CONCERNING TERMINATION AND REASSIGNMENT OF CAPACITY. 
21 
A. 
The October 23 Stipulation provides that a Schedule DCT customer wishing to reduce its 
22 
Contract Capacity may request AEP Ohio to reallocate up to 25% of its Contract Capacity 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 21 of 171
- 20 - 
 
to another Schedule DCT customer subject to certain conditions. 
1 
Q. 
WHY IS THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION’S CAPACITY REASSIGNMENT 
2 
PROVISION INEFFICIENT? 
3 
A. 
While the inclusion of this provision is an improvement over AEP Ohio’s Application, 
4 
which had no such provision, it is nevertheless inefficient to limit the reallocation of 
5 
unneeded capacity to just 25% of a customer’s Contract Capacity.  Reallocating unneeded 
6 
capacity can allow AEP Ohio to serve new large load requests without having to acquire 
7 
new transmission.  Therefore, a greater allowance for reallocation better serves the public 
8 
interest.  While I do not see any reason to limit capacity reassignment at all, in the spirit of 
9 
compromise, the October 10 Stipulation allows reassignment of up to 50% of a customer’s 
10 
Contract Capacity.  As this provision is more efficient than a 25% limitation, it provides a 
11 
greater benefit to customers overall.   
12 
 E.  The October 23 Stipulation Unreasonably Places the Burden on the Schedule 
13 
DCT Customer to Seek Recourse if AEP Ohio Does Not Make Service 
14 
Available to the Customer by the Estimated In-Service Date. 
15 
Q. 
PLEASE DESCRIBE THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION’S PROVISION 
16 
CONCERNING A DCT CUSTOMER’S RECOURSE IN THE EVENT OF A 
17 
DELAY IN PROVIDING SERVICE TO THAT CUSTOMER. 
18 
A. 
The October 23 Stipulation provides that if electric infrastructure is not in place to serve 
19 
the customer by the estimated in-service date, the customer may petition the Commission 
20 
for an adjustment to the contract term based on the facts and circumstances presented at 
21 
that time.    
 
22 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 22 of 171
- 21 - 
 
In contrast, the October 10 Stipulation provides that if electric infrastructure is not 
1 
in place to serve the customer by the estimated in-service date, the end date of the ESA 
2 
would remain in place and not be automatically extended. 
3 
Q. 
WHY IS THE TREATMENT OF A SERVICE DELAY IN THE OCTOBER 10 
4 
STIPULATION MORE REASONABLE THAN THE TREATMENT OF THIS 
5 
ISSUE IN THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION? 
6 
A. 
Large load customers will be investing billions of dollars with the expectation of receiving 
7 
electric service from AEP Ohio.  If service is delayed, the October 23 Stipulation places 
8 
the burden on the DCT customer to seek recourse by petitioning the Commission for any 
9 
changes to the contract term.   Thus, it subjects the large load customer to a potentially 
10 
lengthy process that could also be opposed by the Company – for an issue that the customer 
11 
did not cause.  
12 
Rather than placing the burden on the customer to petition the Commission, the 
13 
October 10 Stipulation achieves a reasonable balancing of interests by simply retaining the 
14 
original end date of the ESA.  This provides an incentive for AEP Ohio to achieve timely 
15 
completion of the required electric infrastructure, while providing assurance to the 
16 
customer that its lengthy contractual commitment will not be further extended due to delays 
17 
in the initiation of service.    
18 
F.  
Various Terms Proposed in the October 23 Stipulation for Completion of an 
19 
LOA and ESA Are Problematic.  
20 
Q. 
ACCORDING TO THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION, HOW MUCH TIME 
21 
WOULD A DCT CUSTOMER HAVE TO SIGN AN LOA AND ESA? 
22 
A. 
Sixty days after AEP Ohio presents them to the customer.  This time frame is problematic 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 23 of 171
- 22 - 
 
if there are terms that need to be worked out between AEP Ohio and the customer. In 
1 
contrast, the October 10 Stipulation provides for ninety days once agreeing to terms with 
2 
AEP Ohio.  Ninety days is a more reasonable turnaround time for a customer that may be 
3 
making investments of hundreds of millions, and perhaps even a billion dollars, such as a 
4 
large data center.  
5 
Q. 
WHAT OTHER PROCESS TERMS IN THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION ARE 
6 
PROBLEMATIC? 
7 
A. 
The land control requirement in Section III.F.1 specifies that in order to request a load 
8 
study, the customer must own, lease or have an option on the land.  While I understand the 
9 
reasoning for this requirement, land purchases can be complex and take time to negotiate.  
10 
Requiring land control to request a study risks delaying projects.  Instead, it would be 
11 
reasonable for the customer to be required to make a demonstration that good faith efforts 
12 
to secure the land are underway. 
13 
In addition, Section III.F.2 indicates that AEP Ohio will group customers into 
14 
tranches when setting up service plans if regional transmission upgrades are needed.  While 
15 
there is a logical basis for this, it is important that such groupings not have the unintended 
16 
consequence of slowing a project down or increasing costs if certain customers in the group 
17 
or tranche elect not to move forward.  There should be reasonable provisions for customers 
18 
who wish to proceed if others in the tranche do not. 
19 
G.  The October 23 Stipulation Unreasonably Subjects Data Centers to Different 
20 
Standards for Installing Behind-the-Meter Generation Than Other Similarly-
21 
Situated Customers.  
22 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 24 of 171
- 23 - 
 
Q. 
PLEASE DESCRIBE THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION’S PROVISION 
1 
CONCERNING BEHIND-THE-METER GENERATION. 
2 
A. 
The October 23 Stipulation requires a Schedule DCT customer that uses behind-the-meter 
3 
generation to offset its Contract Capacity to install and maintain equipment that will 
4 
instantaneously curtail load equal to or greater than the behind-the-meter generation output 
5 
in order to ensure that the customer does not exceed its Contract Capacity in the event that 
6 
its behind-the-meter generation experiences an outage or is otherwise curtailed. 
7 
Q. 
WHAT IS YOUR CONCERN WITH THIS PROVISION? 
8 
A. 
My concern is not directed to the substance of the proposed provision, but rather its 
9 
discriminatory application to data centers.  To the extent that AEP Ohio has a concern with 
10 
customers that have behind-the-meter generation exceeding their Contract Capacity when 
11 
their behind-the-meter generation is down, AEP Ohio should address the issue in a generic 
12 
manner rather than through a provision that targets a single industry.   
13 
In contrast, the October 10 Stipulation provides that new large loads (Schedule EIC) 
14 
may interconnect behind-the-meter generation on the same terms and interconnection rules 
15 
as any other customer.  This non-discriminatory approach is a more reasonable standard 
16 
for the Commission to adopt. 
17 
Q. 
REGARDING THE THIRD CRITERION FOR EVALUATING STIPULATIONS, 
18 
DOES THE OCTOBER 23 STIPULATION VIOLATE ANY IMPORTANT 
19 
REGULATORY PRINCIPLES OR PRACTICES? 
20 
A. 
Yes.   As I discussed at length regarding the second criterion, the October 23 Stipulation 
21 
unduly discriminates against data center customers by mandating tariff terms such as 
22 
contract length, minimum demand charges, and collateral that are disadvantageous relative 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 25 of 171
- 24 - 
 
to the otherwise applicable rate schedule, General Service.  These disadvantageous terms 
1 
are unduly discriminatory because they are only required of customers engaged in a 
2 
specific business activity rather than applied to customers with similar load characteristics 
3 
such as size or load shape.   Based on my experience in ratemaking, I believe this undue 
4 
discrimination constitutes a violation of regulatory principles and practice.  
5 
Further, the excessive minimum demand charges proposed in the October 23 
6 
Stipulation, also discussed at length previously in my testimony, is inconsistent with the 
7 
regulatory principle of aligning costs with cost causation.     
8 
At a minimum for these reasons, the October 23 Stipulation fails to satisfy the third 
9 
prong of the Commission’s three-prong test for the reasonableness of a Stipulation.  
10 
Q. 
DOES THIS CONCLUDE YOUR TESTIMONY IN OPPOSITION? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  
 
12

Term of Contract 
Contracts under this schedule will be made for not less than one (1) year and shall continue thereafter 
until terminated sixty (60) days after either party has given written notice to the other of the intention to 
terminate the agreement. The Company may, at its option, require a longer initial term of contract to fulfill 
the terms and conditions of service and/or in order to protect the Company's ability to recover its 
investment of costs over a reasonable period of time. 
Special Terms and Conditions 
This schedule is subject to the Company’s Terms and Conditions of Service. 
The customer shall provide such cleared rights-of-way, licenses and permits as may be required to 
enable the Company to supply the service applied for. Customer shall maintain clear rights-of-way. 
Service will not be provided hereunder if in the judgment of the Company a danger or nuisance will be 
created thereby. Service will be terminated if after installation it proves to be a danger or nuisance. 
Rates contained herein are based upon continuous use of facilities and are not applicable to seasonal 
use. 
Relocation and Removal of Lamps 
Lamps may be relocated or removed when requested in writing by a proper representative of the 
customer, subject to the following conditions: 
Lamps will be relocated upon payment by the customer of the estimated cost of doing the work. 
Lamps will be removed upon payment by the customer of the estimated cost of doing the work plus the 
undepreciated investment less salvage value of the lamps removed.  
Upon completion of the work, billing for relocation or removal of lamps will be adjusted to reflect actual 
costs. 
The Company will remove or relocate lamps as rapidly as labor conditions will permit.

Availability of Service 
The Company plans to implement LED lights in place of existing street lights over a 5-year transition 
period. Available for street lighting service to municipalities, townships, counties and other governmental 
subdivisions supplied through Company-owned facilities and who request and receive electric generation 
service from either a SSO or a qualified CRES Provider. Service rendered hereunder is predicated upon 
the existence of a valid agreement between the Company and the Customer specifying the type, number 
and location of lamps to be supplied and lighted. This service is not available for non-roadway lighting. 
Service to be Rendered 
The Company agrees to provide and maintain a street lighting system for the customer, together with 
electric energy through a general system of overhead distribution sufficient to continuously operate the 
lamps to give the maximum amount of illumination obtainable under commercial conditions from one-half 
hour after sunset until one-half hour before sunrise, every night and all night, approximately 4,000 hours 
per annum. An underground system of distribution will be provided at prices applicable to overhead 
distribution where the customer pays for trenching and backfilling or provides underground ducts 
designed to Company specification.

Relocation and Removal of Lamps 
Lamps may be relocated or removed when requested in writing by a proper representative of the 
customer, subject to the following conditions: 
Lamps will be relocated upon payment by the customer of the estimated cost of doing the work. 
Lamps will be removed upon payment by the customer of the estimated cost of doing the work plus the 
undepreciated investment less salvage value of the lamps removed.  
Upon completion of the work, billing for relocation or removal of lamps will be adjusted to reflect actual 
costs. 
The Company will remove or relocate lamps as rapidly as labor conditions will permit. 
Temporary Disconnection of Lamps 
The Company will for a period of up to six (6) months temporary disconnect a lamp for a one-time 
distribution charge of $17.18 plus the otherwise applicable monthly fixed distribution charges as stated 
above. Charges for transmission and generation service will not apply during the temporary disconnection 
period. LED facilities equipped with smart nodes will not be subject to the one-time fixed distribution 
charge of $17.18. 
If within the six (6) months’ period the customer requests to have the lamp reconnected, a distribution 
reconnection charge of $17.18 shall be made and the lamp will be billed at the monthly rate charged prior 
to disconnection. LED facilities equipped with smart nodes will not be subject to the one-time fixed 
distribution charge of $17.18. Disconnected lamps may be removed at the Company’s discretion after six 
months and new installations will be billed at rates applicable to new installations. 
Lamp Outages 
For all aggregate outages of four (4) or more in any month which are reported in writing within ten (10) 
days of the end of the month to the Company by a proper representative of the customer, there shall be a 
pro-rata reduction from the bill to reflect such outage.

Delayed Payment Charge 
Bills are due and payable in full by mail, checkless payment plan, electronic payment plan or at an

Metered Voltage 
The rates set forth in this schedule are based upon the delivery and measurement of energy at the same 
voltage, thus measurement will be made at or compensated to the delivery voltage. At the sole discretion 
of the Company, such compensation may be achieved through the use of loss compensating equipment, 
the use of formulas to calculate losses or the application of multipliers to the metered quantities. In such 
cases, the metered KWH values will be adjusted for billing purposes. If the Company elects to adjust 
KWH based on multipliers, the adjustment shall be in accordance with the following: 
(a)
Measurement taken at the low-side of a customer-owned transformer will be multiplied by 1.01.
(b)
Measurements taken at the high-side of a Company-owned transformer will be multiplied by 0.98.

SCHEDULE NEMS 
(Net Energy Metering Service) 
 
 
 
Filed pursuant to Order dated November 17, 2021 in Case No. 20-585-EL-AIR 
  
Issued: November 22, 2021                                               
       Effective: December 1, 2021  
Issued by 
Marc Reitter, President 
AEP Ohio 
 
Availability of Service 
 
This schedule is available to customers with qualifying customer-generator facilities designed to operate 
in parallel with the Company’s system. Customers served under this schedule must also take service 
under the applicable standard service schedule. 
  
Conditions of Service 
 
1. 
A qualifying customer is one whose generating facility complies with all the following 
requirements: 
 
a. 
is fueled by solar, wind, biomass, landfill gas, or hydropower, or uses a microturbine or a 
fuel cell; 
b. 
is located on the customer-generator’s premises; 
c. 
is designed and installed to operate in parallel with the Company’s system without 
adversely affecting the operation of equipment and service of the Company and its 
customers and without presenting safety hazards to Company and customer personnel; 
and   
d. 
is intended primarily to offset part or all of the customer-generator’s electricity needs. 
 
2. 
The customer’s generating equipment shall be installed in accordance with the manufacturer’s 
specifications as well as all applicable provisions of the National Electrical Code. All equipment 
and installations shall comply with all applicable safety and performance standards established by 
the National Electrical Code, the Institute of Electrical and Electronic Engineers and Underwriters 
Laboratories, as well as any additional control and testing requirements adopted by the 
Commission. 
 
Metering 
 
Net energy metering shall be accomplished using a single meter capable of registering the flow of 
electricity in each direction. If the existing electrical meter installed at the customer’s facility is not capable 
of measuring the flow of electricity in two directions, the Company, upon written request of the customer, 
shall install at the customer’s expense an appropriate meter with such capability.  
 
The Company may, at its own expense and with written consent of the customer,  install one or more 
additional meters to monitor the flow of electricity. 
 
Monthly Charges 
 
Monthly charges for energy, and demand where applicable, to serve the customer’s net or total load shall 
be determined according to the Company’s standard service schedule under which the customer would 
otherwise be served, absent the customer-owned generation source. If a non-residential customer’s 
qualifying customer-generator facility has a total rated generating capacity of more than 10 kW, service 
shall be provided under the Company’s general service schedule.  
 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 51 of 171
OHIO POWER COMPANY 
Original Sheet No. 261-2 
 
P.U.C.O. NO. 21 
 
SCHEDULE NEMS 
(Net Energy Metering Service) 
 
 
 
Filed pursuant to Order dated November 17, 2021 in Case No. 20-585-EL-AIR 
  
Issued: November 22, 2021                                               
       Effective: December 1, 2021  
Issued by 
Marc Reitter, President 
AEP Ohio 
 
If the Company supplies more kWh of electricity to the customer than the customer-generator’s facility 
feeds back to the Company’s system during the billing period, all energy charges of the customer’s 
standard service schedule shall be calculated using the customer’s net energy usage for the billing 
period.  
 
If the customer-generator’s facility feeds more kWh of electricity back to the Company’s system than the 
Company supplies to the customer during the billing period, only the generation-related energy charges of 
the customer’s standard service schedule, including all applicable generation-related riders, shall be 
calculated using the customer’s net energy supplied to the Company. All other energy charges shall be 
calculated using an energy value of zero (0) kWh. If the customer’s net billing under the standard service 
schedule is negative during the billing period, the negative net billing shall be allowed to accumulate as a 
credit to offset billing in the next billing period. The customer may request, in writing, a refund of 
accumulated credit that is no greater than an annual true-up of accumulated credits over a twelve month 
period.  
 
Special Terms and Conditions 
 
This schedule is subject to the Company’s Terms and Conditions of Service and all provisions of the 
standard service schedule under which the customer takes service. This schedule is also subject to the 
applicable provisions of the Company’s Minimum Requirements for Distribution System Interconnection.

Schedule GSP 
 
(Generation Station Power) 
 
 
 
Filed pursuant to Order dated November 17, 2021 in Case No. 20-585-EL-AIR 
Issued:  November 22, 2021 
Effective:  December 1, 2021 
Issued By 
Marc Reitter, President 
AEP Ohio 
 
Availability of Service 
 
Service pursuant to this schedule is available to customers that (a) intend to schedule, deliver and sell power 
solely at wholesale, and (b) do not serve load unrelated to the generation facility and the associated facilities 
and electrical equipment located on-site. This Schedule does not apply to generating facilities that are eligible 
for and have elected to participate in remote self-supply under the PJM Open Access Transmission Tariff. The 
generator must have an interconnection agreement under the PJM Open Access Transmission Tariff for service 
at a nominal system voltage of 69,000 volts or higher. 
 
Term of Contract 
 
Contracts under the Schedule shall be made for an initial period of not less than 1 year and shall remain in 
effect, unless terminated by either party by providing written notice to the other party no later than ninety (90) 
days prior to the date of termination. 
 
Monthly Rate 
 
Billing under Schedule GSP will be based on the Company’s General Service Schedule, subject to the 
amendments and exceptions identified herein. 
 
Net Generator 
 
For any calendar month in which the generating facility delivers more energy to the Company’s system 
(generation) than it receives from the Company’s system (use), as measured by generator’s PJM revenue 
meter(s) or the Company meter(s) (as applicable), the customer shall be billed for zero kW demand, zero 
KVAR reactive demand, and zero net kWh use under the provisions of the General Service Schedule and 
applicable riders; provided, however, the following provisions of the General Service Schedule and riders 
shall not apply: the Minimum Charge and Monthly Billing Demand provisions, Generation Energy Rider, 
Generation Capacity Rider, Auction Cost Reconciliation Rider, Basic Transmission Cost Rider, and 
Alternative Energy Rider. 
 
The Company, as PJM metering agent for customer’s generation facility, will report all electricity generation 
and electricity use during the month to PJM as wholesale positive and negative generation, respectively, for 
settlement purposes. 1 
 
Net Consumer 
 
For any calendar month in which the generating facility receives more energy from the Company’s system 
(use) than it delivers to the Company’s system (generation), as measured by generator’s PJM revenue 
meter(s) or the Company meter(s) (as applicable), the customer will be billed for monthly kW demand and for 
net kWh use, in accordance with the provisions of Schedule GS, and billing demand in kW shall be take each 
month as the single highest 30-minute integrated peak in kW during net consumption intervals. 
 
 
1 See, e.g., PJM Interconnection, LLC, Open Access Transmission Tariff, Attachment K. 
 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 54 of 171
OHIO POWER COMPANY 
 
Original Sheet No. 260-2 
 
 
 
P.U.C.O. NO. 21 
 
 
Schedule GSP 
 
(Generation Station Power) 
 
 
 
Filed pursuant to Order dated November 17, 2021 in Case No. 20-585-EL-AIR 
Issued:  November 22, 2021 
Effective:  December 1, 2021 
Issued By 
Marc Reitter, President 
AEP Ohio 
 
The Company, as PJM metering agent for customer’s generating facility, will report to PJM for settlement 
purposes2 all electricity generation during the month as wholesale positive generation and all electricity usage 
as negative generation. As part of that settlement process, any net use as determined for the month (the 
amount by which electricity usage exceeds electricity generation) shall be part of the Company’s retail load. 
 
Special Terms and Conditions 
 
This schedule is subject to the Company’s Terms and Conditions of service. 
 
Consistent with Section 3.0 of the Terms and Conditions of Service, when the customer has two (2) or more 
existing metering points at the site of a generating facility, the Company shall combine all of the metering for 
those points for calculations and billing under this schedule. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2 See, e.g., PJM Interconnection, LLC, Open Access Transmission Tariff, Attachment K.

SCHEDULE COGEN/SPP 
(Cogeneration and/or Small Power Production) 
 
Filed pursuant to Order dated April 17, 2024, in Case No. 20-963-EL-ATA 
 
Issued: April 22, 2024 
 
 
Effective: May 1, 2024 
Issued by 
Marc Reitter, President 
AEP Ohio 
 
Availability of Service 
 
This schedule is available to: 
 
(1) customers with cogeneration and/or small power production (COGEN/SPP) facilities which 
qualify under Section 210 of the Public Utility Regulatory Policies Act of 1978, Cogeneration 
facilities must have a net power production capacity at or below 20 MW, and small power 
production facilities must have a net power production capacity at or below 5 MW.  
(2) customers 
with 
COGEN/SPP 
facilities 
that 
successfully 
rebut 
the 
presumption 
of 
nondiscriminatory access to wholesale markets, as authorized by FERC; and 
(3) customers with COGEN/SPP facilities for which FERC reinstates AEP Ohio’s obligation to 
purchase energy in accordance with 18 CFR § 292.311. 
 
All capacity values are measured in alternating current (AC). Such facilities shall be designed to operate 
properly in parallel with the Company's system without adversely affecting the operation of equipment 
and services of the Company and its customers, and without presenting safety hazards to the Company 
and customer personnel. The customer has the following options under this Schedule: 
  
Option 1 -  The customer does not sell any energy to the Company, and purchases from the 
Company its net load requirements, as determined by appropriate meters located at one 
delivery point. 
 
Option 2 -  The customer sells to the Company the energy produced by the customer's qualifying 
COGEN/SPP facilities in excess of the customer's total load, and purchases from the 
Company its net load requirements, if any, as determined by appropriate meters located 
at one delivery point. 
 
Option 3 -  The customer sells to the Company the total energy produced by the customer's 
qualifying COGEN/SPP facilities, while simultaneously purchasing from the Company its 
total load requirements under the applicable rate schedule, as determined by appropriate 
meters located at one delivery point. 
 
Monthly Charges for Delivery from the Company to the Customer 
Supplemental Service 
 
Available to the customer to supplement another source of power supply which will enable either or both 
sources of supply to be utilized for all or any part of the customer's total requirements. 
 
Charges for energy, and demand where applicable, to serve the customer's net or total load shall be 
determined according to the supplemental service schedule established under the applicable Schedule. 
Option 1 and Option 2 customers with COGEN/SPP facilities having a total design capacity of more than 
10 KW shall receive supplemental service under the general service schedule. 
 
Local Facilities Charge 
 
All Distributed Generation requirements including local facilities charges are subject to the Company’s 
Minimum Requirements for Interconnection Service and Ohio Administrative Code 4901:1-22 (OAC). 
 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 57 of 171
OHIO POWER COMPANY 
 
2nd Revised Sheet No: 263-2 
Cancels 1st Revised Sheet No. 263-2 
P.U.C.O. NO. 21 
 
SCHEDULE COGEN/SPP 
(Cogeneration and/or Small Power Production) 
 
Filed pursuant to Order dated April 17, 2024, in Case No. 20-963-EL-ATA 
 
Issued: April 22, 2024 
 
 
Effective: May 1, 2024 
Issued by 
Marc Reitter, President 
AEP Ohio 
 
The cost of any meter alteration such as but not limited to meter reprogramming, installation of interval 
data recorders etc. shall be paid by the customer as part of the Local Facilities Charge. Any COGEN/SPP 
facility greater than 100 kW will require an interval meter. 
 
Under Option 3, when metering voltage for COGEN/SPP facilities is the same as the Company’s delivery 
voltage, the customer shall, at the customer’s option, either route the COGEN/SPP totalized output leads 
through the metering point, or make available at the metering point for the use of the Company and, as 
specified by the Company, metering current leads which will enable the Company to measure adequately 
the total electrical energy produced by the qualifying COGEN/SPP facilities , as well as to measure the 
electrical energy consumption and capacity requirements of the customer’s total load. When metering 
voltage for COGEN/SPP facilities is different from the Company’s delivery voltage, metering requirements 
and charges shall be determined specifically for each case. 
 
Monthly Credits or Payments for Energy Deliveries 
COGEN/SPP Facilities of 100 kW or less 
 
The rate to be applied to the electrical energy delivered to the Company shall be the average annual total 
real-time Locational Marginal Price (LMP) at the AEP Ohio residual aggregate pricing node less the 
applicable average PJM Operating Reserve Balancing Charge for the billing year. The LMP shall be the 
average annual rate for the twelve month period ended of April of the previous year. The average rate 
shall be adjusted by the Seasonal Winter and Summer Billing Factors as calculated in the Company’s 
Standard Service Offer Master Supply Agreement. 
 
Winter………………………………..5.266¢ per KWH 
Summer……………………………..8.742¢ per KWH 
 
Seasonal Periods 
 
The winter period shall be the billing months of October through May and the summer period shall be the 
billing months of June through September. 
 
The charges computed under this schedule shall be adjusted annually on June 1 of each year. 
 
COGEN/SPP Facilities Greater Than 100 kW 
 
The payment for the electrical energy delivered to the Company shall be computed using the real-time 
hourly price (LMP) at the AEP Ohio residual aggregate pricing node. The customer shall also pay for 
applicable PJM Operating Reserve Balancing Charges, which will be made available upon request. If the 
actual LMP is not available at the time of billing, the bill will be estimated and trued up when that actual 
LMP becomes available. 
 
Terms 
 
Customers on this schedule will receive a monthly invoice that nets the customer’s energy credits with all 
other charges. If the monthly invoice results in an overall credit to the customer and is less than $100, 
then no payment will be issued and the credit will be applied to the next monthly invoice. If the monthly 
invoice results in an overall credit to the customer and is more than $100, residential customers will be 
issued a payment in the form of a debit card and nonresidential customers will receive payment in the

Applicable Riders. 
 
Monthly charges computed under this tariff shall be adjusted in accordance with the applicable Commission-
approved rider(s) listed on Sheet No. 44. 
 
 
Delayed Payment Charge. 
 
All bills under this schedule shall be rendered and due monthly.  If not paid within 17 days after the bill is 
mailed, there shall be added to bills of $3 or less, 10 percent of the amount of the bill; and to bills in excess of 
$3, there shall be added 10 percent of the first $3, plus 3 percent of the amount of the bill in excess of $3. 
 
 
Monthly Billing Demand. 
 
The billing demands in kW for each plant shall be taken each month as the single-highest 15-minute 
integrated peak in kW, as registered at such plant during the month by a demand meter or indicator, subject 
to the off-peak hour provision, but the monthly demand so established shall in no event be less than 60 
percent of the greater of (a) the customer's contract capacity or (b) the customer's highest previously 
established monthly billing demand during the past 11 months or (c) 1,000 kW.  The Metered Voltage 
adjustment, as set forth below, shall not apply to the customer's minimum monthly billing demand. 
 
 
Off-Peak Hour Provision. 
 
Demand created during the off-peak hours (as set forth below) shall be disregarded for billing purposes 
provided that the billing demand shall not be less than 60 percent of the maximum demand created during the 
billing month nor less than 60 percent of either (a) the contract capacity or (b) the customer's highest 
previously established monthly billing demand during the past 11 months. 
 
For the purpose of this provision, the on-peak billing period is defined as 7 a.m. to 9 p.m., local time, Monday 
through Friday.  The off-peak billing period is defined as those hours not designated as on-peak hours.

Adjustments to Rate. 
 
Bills computed under the rates set forth herein will be adjusted as follows: 
 
Metered Voltage 
 
The rates set forth in this tariff are based upon the delivery and measurement of energy at the 
same voltage, thus measurement will be made at or compensated to the delivery voltage.  At the 
sole discretion of the Company, such compensation may be achieved through the use of loss-
compensating equipment, the use of formulas to calculate losses, or the application of multipliers 
to the metered quantities.  In such cases, the metered kWh, kVAr values will be adjusted for 
billing purposes.  If the Company elects to adjust kWh, kW and kVAr based on multipliers, the 
adjustment shall be in accordance with the following: 
 
 
(1) 
Measurements taken at the low-side of a customer-owned transformer will be 
multiplied by 1.01. 
 
(2) 
Measurements taken at the high-side of a Company-owned transformer will be 
multiplied by 0.98. 
 
 
Terms of Contract. 
 
Contracts under this tariff will be made for an initial period of not less than two years and shall remain in effect 
thereafter until either party shall give at least one year's written notice to the other of the intention to 
discontinue service under the terms of this tariff.  Where new facilities are required, the Company reserves 
the right to require initial contracts for periods of greater than two years. 
 
A new initial contract period will not be required for existing customers who increase their contract 
requirements after the original initial period unless new or additional facilities are required. 
 
The Company shall not be required to supply capacity in excess of that contracted for except by mutual 
agreement.

Special Terms and Conditions. 
 
This tariff is subject to the Company's Terms and Conditions of Service. 
 
This tariff is also available to customers having other sources of energy supply who purchase standby or 
backup service from the Company.  Where such conditions exist, the customer shall contract for the 
maximum amount of demand in kW which the Company might be required to furnish, but not less than 1,000 
kW. The Company shall not be obligated to supply demands in excess of that contracted for.   
 
 
Customers with cogeneration and/or small power production facilities shall take service under Rider NMS (Net 
Metering Service Rider), Tariff COGEN/SPP or by special agreement with the Company. 
 
Special Terms and Conditions for Customer over 150 MW. 
 
These provisions apply to customers whose contract capacity exceeds 150 MW or is reasonably expected to 
grow to exceed 150 MW at one or more aggregated premises, each of 1 MW or larger.  Company will 
exercise reasonable discretion when choosing to aggregate premises, with such discretion based on factors 
including, but not limited to, premises sharing one or more of: common owner(s), a common parent company, 
common local electrical infrastructure, and common control. 
 
Contracts will be made for an initial period of twenty (20) years and shall remain in effect thereafter unless 
cancelled or modified pursuant to the terms hereunder.  Either party shall give at least five years’ written 
notice to the other of the intention to discontinue service under the terms of this tariff.  Such notice shall not 
reduce the twenty (20) year initial term.   
 
In the event of a permanent closure by the customer occurring after the first five (5) years of the initial contract 
term, the customer may terminate the contract by providing a one-time payment, at the time service ends, 
equal to five (5) years of minimum billing under this tariff.  In the event of a permanent closure, the customer 
shall notify the Company within three (3) business days of making this determination. 
 
The customer shall give at least five (5) years’ prior written notice to the Company of the intention to reduce 
the contract capacity specified in the contract, unless the parties mutually agree to a shorter period of notice.  
Such notice shall not reduce the maximum contract capacity established during the term of the contract by 
more than twenty (20) percent, except by mutual agreement.

the customer’s previous maximum monthly non-fuel bill.  The amount of collateral under the foregoing 
calculation will be recomputed annually, and the customer shall have to provide the recomputed amount if it is 
10% or more greater than the current amount held.

SCHEDULE L.C.P. 
(Large Capacity Power Service) 
AVAILABILITY OF SERVICE 
Available for general service to customers. Customers shall contract for a definite amount of electrical capacity in 
kilowatts, which shall be sufficient to meet maximum requirements, but in no case shall the contract capacity be less than 1,000 
kW. 
MONTHLY RATE

SCHEDULE L.C.P. 
(Large Capacity Power Service) 
(continued) 
MEASUREMENT AND DETERMINATION OF DEMAND AND ENERGY 
The billing demand in KW shall be taken each month as the single highest 30-minute peak in KW as registered during 
the month in the on-peak period by a demand meter or indicator. The monthly billing demand established hereunder shall not 
be less than 60% of the greater of (a) the customer's contract capacity or (b) the customer's highest previously established 
monthly billing demand during the past 11 months. 
The off-peak excess demand shall be the amount by which the demand created during the off-peak period exceeds the 
monthly billing demand. 
The reactive demand in KVAR shall be taken each month as the single highest 30-minute peak in KVAR as registered 
during the month by a demand meter or indicator. 
Billing demands shall be rounded to the nearest whole KW and KVAR. 
For the purpose of this Schedule, the on-peak billing period is defined as 7 a.m. to 9 p.m., local time, for all 
weekdays, Monday through Friday. The off-peak billing period is defined as 9 p.m. to 7 a.m., local time, for all weekdays, all 
hours of the day on Saturdays and Sundays, and the legally observed holidays of New Year's Day, Memorial Day, 
Independence Day, Labor Day, Thanksgiving Day and Christmas Day. 
METERED VOLTAGE ADJUSTMENT 
The rates set forth in this Schedule are based upon delivery and measurement of energy at the same voltage. When 
the measurement of energy occurs at a voltage different than the delivery voltage, the measurement of energy will be 
compensated to the delivery voltage. At the sole discretion of the Company, such compensation may be achieved through the 
use of loss compensating equipment or the application of multipliers to the metered quantities. In such cases, metered KWH, 
KW and KVAR will be adjusted for billing purposes. In cases where multipliers are used to adjust metered usage, the 
adjustment shall be as follows: 
(a) 
Measurements taken at the low-side of a customer-owned transformer will be multiplied by 1.01 
(b) 
Measurements taken at the high-side of a Company-owned transformer will be multiplied by 0.98. 
TERM 
The customer shall contract for a definite amount of electrical capacity in kilowatts sufficient to meet its maximum 
requirements, but in no event shall the contract capacity be less than 1,000 KW. 
Contracts will be required for an initial period of not less than two (2) years and shall remain in effect thereafter until 
either party shall give the other at least one (1) year's written notice ofthe intention to discontinue service under this Schedule. 
A new initial contract period will not be required for existing customers who change their contract requirements aRer 
the original initial period unless new or additional facilities are required. The Company reserves the right to make initial 
contracts for periods longer than two (2) years pursuant to the Extension of Service provision of the Company's Terms and 
Conditions of Service. 
The Company shall not be required to supply capacity in excess of that contracted for except by mutual agreement.

(N) The following terms and conditions apply to Customers over 200 MW: 
Contracts will be made for an initial period of twentv (20) years and shall remain in effect thereafter unless 
cancelled or modified pursuant to the terms hereunder. 
Either party shall give at least five years’ written notice to the other of the intention to discontinue service under 
the terms of this Schedule. Such notice shall not reduce the twenty-year initial term. In the event of a permanent closure by 
the customer occurring after the first five (5) years of the initial contract term. the customer may exit the contract by 
providing a one-time payment equal to five (5) years of minimum billing under this Schedule. 
The customer shall give at least five (5) years’ written notice to the Company of the intention to reduce the contract 
capacity specified in the contract, but any contract capacitv changes may be implemented with less than five (5) years’ 
notice with mutual agreement. Such notice shall not reduce the maximum contract capacity established during the term of 
the contract by more than 20%. except by mutual agreement. Such notice shall not be given during the first five ( 5 )  vears of 
the initial contract term. 
In addition to the Measurement and Determination of Demand and Energy provisions, the customer’s monthly 
billing demand shall in no event be less than 90 percent of the greater of (a) the customer’s on-peak contract capacity or 
[b) the customer’s highest previously established monthly billing demand during the past 11 months or (c) the customer’s 
maximum demand created during the billing month. 
The customer shall provide collateral in a form acceptable to the Company based upon the creditworthiness of the 
customer. The amount of collateral provided shall be eaual to 24 times the customer’s previous maximum monthly non- 
fuel bill. During the first year of the contract, the maximum expected bill for the year shall be used. The amount of collateral 
to be provided will be recomputed annuallv, and updated if the recomimted value is 10% greater than the current amount 
held.

The Interim Pilot 1CP Demand rates for the Basic Transmission Cost Rider are limited to availability as 
established in Case No. 23-23-EL-SSO. The monthly $/kW for the Interim Pilot 1CP Basic Transmission rate will 
be based on the participating customer’s demand during the single zonal transmission peak. The 1CP will be 
changed each January based on the customer’s contribution to the single zonal transmission peak during the 
previous year.  
 
Consistent with the terms and conditions adopted in the Opinion and Order in Case No. 23-23-EL-SSO, this pilot 
program is created to include a Pilot 6CP Demand rates for the Basic Transmission Cost Rider. The monthly 
$/kW for the interim Pilot 6CP Basic Transmission rate will be based on the participating customer’s average 
hourly load coincident with the hourly peaks for AEP Retail Load (AEP East transmission zone) during the months 
of December, January, February, June, July, and August (in accordance with AEP’s Transmission Agreement for 
each year November 1 through October 31), and grossed up for losses. The 6CP will be changed each January 
based on the customer’s contribution to the six zonal transmission peaks during the previous year.  
 
This Rider is subject to reconciliation, including, but not limited to, refunds to customers, based upon the flow 
through impact to this Rider of changes to the Open Access Transmission Tariff approved by the Federal Energy 
Regulatory Commission (including changes related to the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017) or based upon the 
results of audits ordered by the Commission.

6 
II.  
OVERVIEW AND CONCLUSIONS 
7 
Q. 
WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF YOUR TESTIMONY IN THIS PROCEEDING? 
8 
A. 
My testimony responds to the Application by AEP Ohio (or “Company”) to establish a 
9 
tariff (“Schedule DCP (Data Center Power)”)1 applicable to data center customers. 
10 
Q. 
WHAT ARE YOUR PRIMARY CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS? 
11 
A. 
I offer the following conclusions and recommendations:   
12 
 
 
In response to the load-growth-related issues AEP Ohio raises, it is reasonable for 
13 
the Commission to adopt policies that accommodate load growth in a thoughtful and 
14 
deliberate manner so as to avoid cost shifting if investments are made to serve new load 
15 
that does not fully materialize, but also to avoid imposing unjust and discriminatory terms 
16 
on an important growing industry that has the potential to place downward pressure on 
17 
rates over time.  To that end: 
18 
 
 
1.  It is reasonable for the Commission to require new large loads of 50 MW or 
19 
greater to sign long-term contracts with minimum demand provisions and an associated 
20 
exit fee supported either by sufficient customer capitalization or collateral.  Specifically, I 
21 
recommend the Commission adopt a mandatory minimum contract term for new large 
22 
 
1 Exhibit MSM-1. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 86 of 171
HIGGINS / 5 
loads of eight years, with a 24-month exit notice requirement and a capacity 
1 
reduction/termination fee that is based on the foregone net revenues from the minimum 
2 
demand charge over the remaining contract term after accounting for the release or transfer 
3 
of the terminated capacity to serve new load. In addition, the tariff should permit exiting 
4 
customers to negotiate mutually agreeable exit terms with AEP Ohio. 
5 
 
 
2.  The Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s proposal to structure a tariff change 
6 
based on the business activity of the customer (i.e., data centers) rather than the 
7 
characteristics of the customer’s load.  Instead, the changes to the tariff terms under 
8 
consideration in this docket should be applicable to any new large loads of 50 MW or 
9 
greater on a non-discriminatory basis as to end use.  AEP Ohio can accomplish this by 
10 
making changes to its existing Schedule GS and without creating a novel rate classification. 
11 
 
 
3.  The Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s attempt to “reserve the right” to 
12 
migrate data center customers with signed electric service agreements to the proposed new 
13 
tariff in a later rate proceeding.  
14 
 
 
4.  AEP Ohio’s proposal to increase the minimum billing demand from 60% to 90% 
15 
for data center customers lacks any analytical support in the Company’s filing and the 
16 
Commission should reject it.  I demonstrate in my testimony that an increase in the 
17 
minimum demand charge is unnecessary if new large load customers are required to enter 
18 
into long-term contracts. 
19 
 
 
5.  It is reasonable for AEP Ohio to require that certain new large loads not meeting 
20 
specified capitalization requirements provide collateral to support 50% of the minimum 
21 
billing demand for eight years with a 60% minimum demand charge. For a longer term or 
22 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 87 of 171
HIGGINS / 6 
higher minimum demand charge, I recommend that the Commission scale back the 50% 
1 
financial coverage requirement proportionately. 
2 
 
 
6.  AEP Ohio’s proposal that new data center loads greater than 25 MW be subject 
3 
to emergency interruption is poorly articulated and the Company provides no justification 
4 
for singling out data centers for such interruptions.  The Commission should therefore 
5 
reject this proposed provision.  In addition, AEP Ohio’s proposed language requiring 
6 
customers to follow all applicable technical requirements should not be included in any 
7 
rate schedule changes approved in this case, but instead be relocated to a section of the 
8 
tariff of more general applicability. 
9 
 
 
7.  The Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s proposal to restrict the load ramping 
10 
period for data centers greater than 25 MW to three years because it is both impractical and 
11 
unreasonably restrictive.   
12 
 
 
8.  AEP Ohio should commit to delivery of contract capacity on the schedule 
13 
designated in the electric service agreement.  In the event that AEP Ohio is unable to meet 
14 
the contracted delivery date, remedies should be made available to the customer in the 
15 
tariff.  
16 
 
17 
III.  
BACKGROUND ON THE DATA CENTER COALITION 
18 
Q. 
PLEASE DESCRIBE DCC. 
19 
A. 
DCC is the national membership association for the data center industry, representing 
20 
leading data center owners and operators who maintain data center infrastructure across 
21 
the country and globe. DCC supports the data center community through public policy 
22 
advocacy, thought leadership, stakeholder outreach, and community engagement. DCC 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 88 of 171
HIGGINS / 7 
also advocates for a highly skilled and diverse technology workforce, greater access to 
1 
clean energy, and a competitive business environment to support the growth and success 
2 
of this essential business sector. DCC has 30 members, including companies that own and 
3 
operate data centers in AEP Ohio’s service territory and are customers of AEP Ohio. 
4 
Q. 
WHAT ARE DATA CENTERS? 
5 
A. 
Data centers are facilities that house computing machines and related hardware. They 
6 
provide the essential digital infrastructure that supports the applications, platforms and 
7 
services people rely on every day.   
8 
In 2021, the U.S. digital economy accounted for $3.70 trillion of gross output, $2.41 
9 
trillion of value added (translating to 10.3% of U.S. GDP), $1.24 trillion of compensation, 
10 
and 8 million jobs. This segment of the economy is also growing at nearly double the rate 
11 
than the rest of the economy, with a 9.8% increase in price-adjusted GDP.2 Data centers 
12 
are a critical component of the digital economy and an enabler of its growth and benefits. 
13 
Q. 
WHAT IS DCC’S INTEREST IN THIS PROCEEDING? 
14 
A. 
In this proceeding, AEP Ohio has proposed a tariff that singles out data center customers. 
15 
The proposed DCP tariff includes rates, terms and conditions that substantially depart from 
16 
the Company’s existing industrial tariffs. If approved, the DCP tariff will adversely impact 
17 
planned data center development in the Company’s service territory. More concerning, 
18 
AEP Ohio suggests it may eventually migrate existing customers to its proposed tariff, 
19 
which means existing data center customers (including DCC’s members) may be directly 
20 
and adversely impacted by AEP Ohio’s proposal.  DCC’s members rely on reliable and 
21 
 
2 See Tina Highfill and Christopher Surfield, Bureau of Economic Analysis, “New and Revised Statistics of the U.S. 
Digital Economy, 2005-2021 (Nov. 2022). Available at: https://www.bea.gov/system/files/2022-11/new-and-
revised-statistics-of-the-us-digital-economy-2005-2021.pdf. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 89 of 171
HIGGINS / 8 
affordable electric service and consequently have a substantial interest in ensuring fair and 
1 
comparable service at just and reasonable rates to support their businesses over the short- 
2 
and long-term.   
3 
 
 
4 
IV.  
OVERVIEW OF AEP OHIO’S PROPOSED DATA CENTER TARIFF 
5 
Q. 
PLEASE DESCRIBE AEP OHIO’S PROPOSED DATA CENTER TARIFF. 
6 
A. 
AEP Ohio proposes to apply a new Data Center Power Tariff to data centers with monthly 
7 
demand of more than 25 MW that execute an electric service agreement with the Company 
8 
or expand their existing contract capacity after the effective date of the proposed tariff.  For 
9 
the purpose of assessing demand charges, including those applicable to riders, the tariff 
10 
establishes a monthly minimum billing demand of no less than 90% of the greater of (a) 
11 
the customer's contract capacity, or (b) the customer’s highest previously established 
12 
monthly transmission billing demand during the past 11 months.  The tariff requires 
13 
contracts for an initial term of not less than ten years, commencing upon energization, and 
14 
requires the customer pay an exit fee if the customer chooses to leave early.  Specifically, 
15 
the tariff does not permit the customer to exit its contract for five years and requires the 
16 
customer pay an exit fee equal to three years of minimum contract demand to terminate the 
17 
contract prior to the end of the ten-year period.  Effectively, this results in a minimum 
18 
eight-year financial commitment for 90% of contracted demand.  AEP Ohio indicates that 
19 
it may, at its discretion, allow a three-year “load ramp” – the time between commencement 
20 
of service and until the customer commits to full contract capacity.3  The proposed tariff 
21 
also allows AEP Ohio to determine security and collateral provisions to support 
22 
 
3 In its Application, AEP Ohio indicates that the contract period will include a three-year load ramp if and only if the 
Company agrees to that provision. See Application at Paragraph 27. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 90 of 171
HIGGINS / 9 
enforcement of the tariff provisions and requires that data customers be subject to 
1 
involuntary interruption, apparently prior to other customers, during certain emergency 
2 
events, and would be subject to service disconnection if they do not respond.  Finally, the 
3 
proposed tariff also requires that the customer follow all applicable technical operating 
4 
requirements, such as not intentionally or unintentionally cycling load in a way that creates 
5 
an imbalanced or unacceptable system frequency.  Upon detection of any activities outside 
6 
of the technical requirements, the Company reserves the right to disconnect the customer. 
7 
Q. 
HOW DOES THE PROPOSED DATA CENTER TARIFF DIFFER FROM THE 
8 
CURRENT TARIFF TERMS APPLICABLE TO DATA CENTER CUSTOMERS? 
9 
A. 
Data centers take service on Schedule GS (General Service).  Per the terms of that tariff, 
10 
GS customers enter into one-year contracts and are subject to a 60% minimum demand 
11 
charge.  In contrast, the proposed data center tariff requires a ten-year contract and a 90% 
12 
minimum demand charge, which is a financial obligation that is an order of magnitude 
13 
greater than GS service.  In addition, the proposed data center tariff requires that customers 
14 
either meet certain capitalization thresholds or provide material collateral equal to 50% of 
15 
the minimum demand charge for the full ten-year term, a provision that is not otherwise 
16 
required for GS customers.  The exit fee requirements and involuntary interruption 
17 
provisions are unique to the proposed data center tariff.   
18 
Q. 
WHAT IS AEP OHIO’S JUSTIFICATION FOR ITS PROPOSAL? 
19 
A. 
AEP Ohio contends that its proposed data center tariff is needed to respond to 
20 
unprecedented load growth by data centers in the AEP Ohio service territory, especially in 
21 
central Ohio.  The Company states that it has signed letters of agreements or electric service 
22 
agreements with data center customers that will more than double the amount of load in 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 91 of 171
HIGGINS / 10 
central Ohio by 2030, taking it from 4,000 MW to 9,000 MW.4  Further, the Company 
1 
contends that it has received additional requests from more than 50 customers to reserve 
2 
capacity for new or expanded load totaling more than 30,000 MW.5  Meanwhile, according 
3 
to AEP Ohio witness Mr. Kamran Ali, the Company’s infrastructure analysis indicates that 
4 
significant infrastructure enhancements will be needed to meet demand in excess of 
5 
approximately 10,000 MW in Central Ohio.6  AEP Ohio further justifies its proposal for a 
6 
data center rate by asserting that data centers create fewer permanent jobs than industrial 
7 
customers of a similar load size.7  
8 
 
 
AEP Ohio does not offer any analytical justification for the specific proposed terms 
9 
in the data center tariff, including the 90% minimum demand charge, ten-year contract 
10 
term, collateral requirements, or exit fee.  Instead, AEP Ohio generally asserts that its 
11 
proposal is intended to elicit a long-term capacity commitment from new large data center 
12 
loads to help ensure the capacity expansion is needed at the level requested and to help 
13 
justify the time and cost associated with building additional high-voltage transmission to 
14 
serve these new customers.  
15 
 
16 
V.  
RESPONSE TO AEP OHIO’S PROPOSAL 
17 
Q. 
WHAT IS YOUR OVERALL REACTION TO AEP OHIO’S PROPOSED DATA 
18 
CENTER POWER TARIFF? 
19 
A. 
I acknowledge that AEP Ohio is experiencing and anticipating a wave of load growth 
20 
driven by data center demand.  As I state above, in response to that wave, it is reasonable 
21 
 
4 Direct Testimony of Matthew M. McKenzie at 5.  
5 Direct Testimony of Lisa O. Kelso at 6. 
6 Direct Testimony of Kamran Ali at 6. 
7 Direct Testimony of Matthew M. McKenzie at 9. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 92 of 171
HIGGINS / 11 
for the Commission to adopt policies that accommodate load growth in a thoughtful and 
1 
deliberate manner.  I agree that it is appropriate to implement measures to protect against 
2 
cost impacts that can occur if AEP Ohio makes investments to serve new load that does 
3 
not fully materialize.  At the same time, it is important that the Commission not take actions 
4 
that would depress the growth of an important emerging industry by imposing unjust and 
5 
discriminatory terms.   
6 
 
 
The Company’s proposal to propose a discriminatory tariff applicable specifically 
7 
to data centers is misguided and does not strike the right balance.  A tariff based on business 
8 
activity or end-use, lacking any analytical foundation, is not the right answer.  
9 
Consequently, I recommend the Commission reject AEP Ohio’s proposal as unreasonable 
10 
and unduly discriminatory.  I do not suggest, however, that the Commission should ignore 
11 
AEP Ohio’s concerns. Rather, the Commission should recognize that AEP Ohio’s 
12 
concerns, while valid in certain respects, pertain to any new large load.  Indeed, certain 
13 
elements of AEP Ohio’s proposal are non-objectionable if they are applied on a non-
14 
discriminatory basis. Other elements of the Company’s proposal may be modified to 
15 
provide a balanced and reasonable result.   
16 
Q. 
WHAT ASPECTS OF AEP OHIO’S PROPOSAL WARRANT REJECTION? 
17 
A. 
The Commission should reject the following elements in AEP Ohio’s proposal: 
18 
 
 
1.  AEP Ohio’s proposal to structure the tariff change based on the business activity 
19 
of the customer rather than the characteristics of the customer’s load is meritless and the 
20 
Commission should reject it. 
21 
 
 
2.  AEP Ohio’s attempt to “reserve the right” to migrate data center customers with 
22 
signed electric service agreements to the proposed new tariff in a later rate proceeding is 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 93 of 171
HIGGINS / 12 
unreasonable, unduly discriminatory, and an improper “bait and switch” business practice, 
1 
and the Commission should reject it.  
2 
 
 
3.  AEP Ohio’s proposal to increase the minimum billing demand from 60% to 90% 
3 
for data center customers lacks any analytical support in the Company’s filing.  Later in 
4 
my testimony I demonstrate that an increase in the minimum demand charge is unnecessary 
5 
to mitigate the risk of stranded costs or cost shifts if new large load customers are required 
6 
to enter into long-term contracts. 
7 
 
 
4.  AEP Ohio’s proposal that new data center loads greater than 25 MW be subject 
8 
to emergency interruption is poorly articulated and the Company provides no justification 
9 
for singling out data centers for such interruptions.  As such, the Commission should reject 
10 
this proposed provision.  
11 
 
 
5.  The Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s proposal to restrict the load ramping 
12 
period for data centers greater than 25 MW to three years.   
13 
Q. 
WHAT CONCEPTS IN AEP OHIO’S PROPOSAL HAVE MERIT BUT SHOULD 
14 
BE MODIFIED? 
15 
A. 
The following concepts have in AEP Ohio’s proposal have merit but should be modified 
16 
to achieve a more reasonable and balanced outcome: 
17 
 
 
1.  The Commission should increase the 25 MW threshold for applicability of the 
18 
new tariff terms to 50 MW and apply the new terms to all new large loads on a non-
19 
discriminatory basis, irrespective of customer end-use. 
20 
 
 
2.  It is reasonable for the Commission to require new large loads to sign long-term 
21 
contracts with minimum demand provisions and an associated exit fee.  AEP Ohio proposes 
22 
mandatory ten-year contracts with an exit provision after five years and an associated exit 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 94 of 171
HIGGINS / 13 
fee equal to three years of minimum contract demand payments.  Effectively, this results 
1 
in an eight-year minimum commitment.  I recommend adoption of a mandatory minimum 
2 
contract term for new large loads of eight years, with a 24-month exit notice requirement 
3 
and an exit fee that is based on the foregone net revenues from the minimum demand charge 
4 
over the remaining eight-year term after accounting for the release or transfer of the 
5 
terminated capacity to serve new load. 
6 
 
 
3.  I have no objections to the Company requiring that customers follow all 
7 
applicable technical requirements. However, such a provision more properly belongs in the 
8 
Company’s tariff as a general requirement, and not one that is specific to a particular rate 
9 
schedule.   
10 
Q. 
ARE THERE TERMS IN AEP OHIO’S PROPOSAL WITH WHICH YOU 
11 
GENERALLY AGREE? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The requirement that customers either meet certain capitalization thresholds or 
13 
provide collateral equal to 50% of the minimum charges over the term of the contract, 
14 
applicable to certain customers lacking a sufficiently strong credit rating and sufficient 
15 
liquidity, are reasonable in the context of the eight-year contract term and 60% minimum 
16 
demand charge I am recommending in this case, so long as this requirement is applied to 
17 
all new large loads on a non-discriminatory basis, i.e., this provision does not discriminate 
18 
against data center customers.  For a longer term or higher minimum demand charge, I 
19 
recommend that the Commission scale back the 50% financial coverage proportionately.  
20 
 
 
AEP Ohio proposes several options whereby these requirements can be met: (1) a 
21 
guarantee by the corporate parent or an affiliate with sufficient credit metrics; (2) a standby 
22 
letter of credit; or (3) cash.  These options strike me as generally reasonable.  In addition, 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 95 of 171
HIGGINS / 14 
the amount of the collateral requirement would be reduced by one year’s minimum charges 
1 
for each year the customer is energized and makes on-time payments under the contract, 
2 
another provision that I support. 
3 
Q. 
ARE THERE IMPORTANT CONCEPTS THAT ARE MISSING FROM AEP 
4 
OHIO’S PROPOSED TARIFF? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  AEP Ohio’s proposal is focused entirely on the obligations of data centers that would 
6 
be entering into new electric service agreements and avoids offering any enforceable 
7 
obligations requiring the Company to meet its contractual obligations in a timely manner.  
8 
In the interest of balance and fairness, the Company should also bear responsibilities to the 
9 
customers it is requiring to sign long-term contracts that are backed by substantial customer 
10 
capitalization or collateral. These responsibilities should be better articulated in the tariff.  
11 
Q. 
WILL YOU ADDRESS EACH OF THE ISSUES IDENTIFIED ABOVE IN YOUR 
12 
TESTIMONY? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  I will address them in the discussion that follows. 
14 
A.  The Commission Should Reject AEP Ohio’s Proposal to Discriminate Against 
15 
Customers Based on Business Activity.  
16 
Q. 
IS AEP’S PROPOSAL FOR A TARIFF THAT IS SPECIFIC TO DATA CENTERS 
17 
REASONABLE? 
18 
A. 
No.  The fundamental concerns raised by the Company center around the impacts of 
19 
substantial load growth by very large customers, and not around any impacts specific to 
20 
data center customers.   
21 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 96 of 171
HIGGINS / 15 
Q. 
PLEASE ELABORATE. 
1 
A. 
The Company’s fundamental reason for proposing the tariff terms it advances in this 
2 
proceeding is to mitigate the risk of its retail customers bearing the costs of transmission 
3 
buildout—which could include extra high-voltage transmission lines—required to serve 
4 
large new loads, in the event those loads do not materialize. In other words, the only 
5 
characteristic of data center customers driving the Company’s proposal is the size of those 
6 
customers’ loads. Any other type of customer with loads of a similar magnitude would 
7 
require similar transmission infrastructure, and therefore data centers do not present a 
8 
unique risk to the Company or its retail customers.  
9 
Q. 
HAS 
THE 
COMPANY 
SHOWN 
ANY 
ACTUAL 
OR 
MEASURABLE 
10 
DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THE SERVICE IT PROVIDES DATA CENTER 
11 
CUSTOMERS AND ANY OTHER CUSTOMERS WITH EQUIVALENT OR 
12 
SIMILAR LOADS? 
13 
A. 
No, the Company has not shown any actual or measurable difference between the service 
14 
it provides to data center customers and the service it provides to other customers with 
15 
equivalent or similar loads, and indeed, none exists—the Company provides substantially 
16 
the same service to both. Company witness McKenzie generally asserts data centers present 
17 
the potential for significant, scalable load at single locations, but never explains why or 
18 
how those characteristics relate to the infrastructure or service those customers require, or 
19 
impact the risk the Company is attempting to mitigate in this proceeding.  
20 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 97 of 171
HIGGINS / 16 
Q. 
BUT 
AREN’T 
DATA 
CENTERS 
DRIVING 
THE 
LOAD 
GROWTH 
1 
ANTICIPATED IN AEP OHIO’S SERVICE TERRITORY? 
2 
A. 
I agree that at the current time data center load is driving much of the increase in demand 
3 
for power in AEP Ohio’s service territory.  But growth trends in the load associated with 
4 
any specific customer segment should not drive tariff design.  The Commission should 
5 
refrain from singling out specific industries or end-uses for tariff conditions that are more 
6 
onerous than those imposed on otherwise similarly-situated customers in other industries.   
7 
 
 
Rate classes should be established based on load characteristics rather than end-use 
8 
as load characteristics are the fundamental elements for determining cost causation.  The 
9 
costs caused by a rate class are driven by its aggregate load shape and the volume of power 
10 
it consumes, not the end-use that its power consumption supports.  If it is determined that 
11 
new large loads require special terms to ensure long-term capacity commitments, then such 
12 
terms should apply equally to all new large loads of similar size. Focusing on customer 
13 
segments, as opposed to load characteristics, risks obligating the target customer segment 
14 
to bear disproportionate responsibility for costs driven by a broader set of customers.  
15 
 
 
As such, the Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s attempt to apply its proposed 
16 
tariff specifically to data centers. Any special tariff terms the Commission adopts to 
17 
mitigate the risks described above should apply on a non-discriminatory basis to new large 
18 
loads, irrespective of end-use. 
19 
Q. 
DO YOU HAVE ANYTHING ELSE TO ADD WITH RESPECT TO AEP OHIO’S 
20 
PROPOSAL TO SINGLE OUT DATA CENTERS? 
21 
A. 
Yes. I understand the U.S. Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (“FERC”) recently 
22 
considered and rejected a similar tariff proposal in Docket Nos. ER24-1610-000 and ER-
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 98 of 171
HIGGINS / 17 
24-1610-001.8 In that case, Basin Electric Power Cooperative (“Basin”) proposed revisions 
1 
to its existing tariffs that would add certain rate schedules applicable to all crypto and block 
2 
chain related loads in the service territories of its members. My understanding is that Basin 
3 
believed its proposal was necessary because, according to Basin, the characteristics of 
4 
crypto customers made them more likely to relocate after taking service, creating a risk of 
5 
stranding assets constructed to serve those customers’ significant demands. It is also my 
6 
understanding that FERC rejected the proposal in part because Basin had not shown that 
7 
crypto loads pose a greater stranded asset risk than any other loads of a similar size. I am 
8 
not a lawyer and do not offer any opinion on the precedential effect of FERC’s decision on 
9 
the Commission’s decision in this proceeding, but rather suggest simply that FERC’s 
10 
decision may be of interest to this Commission given the similarities in the issues at play 
11 
in the two proceedings. 
12 
Q. 
IF THE COMMISSION DETERMINES THAT ADDITIONAL TARIFF TERMS 
13 
ARE REQUIRED FOR NEW LARGE LOADS, SHOULD THE TERMS APPLY TO 
14 
ALL NEW LOADS IN EXCESS OF 25 MW? 
15 
A. 
No.  I do not believe that a 25 MW load is substantial enough to warrant inclusion in such 
16 
a tariff change.  I believe that 50 MW is a more appropriate minimum size for any such 
17 
changes to be applicable. 
18 
 
8 188 FERC ¶ 61,132 (issued August 20, 2024). 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 99 of 171
HIGGINS / 18 
Q. 
SHOULD THE COMMISSION GIVE ANY WEIGHT TO AEP OHIO’S 
1 
JUSTIFICATIONS FOR PUTTING NEW LARGE DATA CENTERS IN A 
2 
SEPARATE CLASS FROM OTHER NEW LARGE LOADS? 
3 
A. 
No.  AEP Ohio lists several justifications, none of which warrant establishing a separate 
4 
rate class based on end use.  The Company variously identifies the “newness” of data 
5 
centers, potential size, their scalability, and the observation that data centers are driving 
6 
recent growth.9  As I explained above, AEP Ohio never explains why or how the potential 
7 
size or scalability of data centers relates to the infrastructure or service those customers 
8 
require, or impact the risk the Company is attempting to mitigate in this proceeding. 
9 
 
As I indicated at the outset of my testimony, I do not object to new tariff terms that 
10 
require long-term contracts supported either by substantial capitalization or collateral for 
11 
new large loads of 50 MW or greater.  Certainly, new large data centers would be subject 
12 
to these requirements, which fully addresses AEP Ohio’s concerns regarding size, 
13 
scalability, “newness”, and the relationship to load growth without resorting to a tariff that 
14 
discriminates on the basis of end use. 
15 
Q. 
WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE TO AEP OHIO’S JUSTIFICATION FOR A 
16 
SEPARATE DATA CENTER RATE BASED ON ITS ASSERTIONS THAT DATA 
17 
CENTERS CREATE FEWER PERMANENT JOBS THAN INDUSTRIAL 
18 
CUSTOMERS OF A SIMILAR LOAD SIZE? 
19 
A. 
As a threshold matter, I believe it is wholly improper for a public utility to be proposing 
20 
discriminatory terms that are disadvantageous to customers in any particular industry based 
21 
on the utility’s perception of the job-creating capacity of that industry.  Moreover, the basis 
22 
 
9 Direct Testimony of Matthew M. McKenzie at 7-9. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 100 of 171
HIGGINS / 19 
for AEP Ohio’s contentions concerning the economic impact of the data center industry 
1 
are misguided and fail to take into account the indirect employment and economic impact 
2 
of the service support provided by data centers, not only to American industry, but to the 
3 
public at large through data storage, computing infrastructure, and implementation of data 
4 
protocols.10  AEP Ohio fails to consider that data centers provide the technology that 
5 
virtually every economic entity relies upon to reach customers and operate in the modern 
6 
economy.  And AEP Ohio has certainly made no attempt to analyze the impact of its 
7 
proposal on economic development, data center attraction/retention, associated tax 
8 
revenues, or any related industries or local businesses.11 The Commission should disregard 
9 
any justification offered by AEP Ohio in support of its tariff proposal related to alleged 
10 
employment effects.  
11 
B.  
AEP Ohio Should Not Be Permitted to Migrate Data Center Customers With 
12 
Signed electric service agreements to the Proposed New Tariff in a Later Rate 
13 
Proceeding. 
14 
Q.

15 
CENTER CUSTOMERS OR THOSE WITH SIGNED CONTRACTS TO THE 
16 
NEW RATE SCHEDULE IT IS PROPOSING? 
17 
A. 
No. One of the insidious features of structuring the new tariff as an end-use rate targeting 
18 
data centers is that it could potentially be used in the future to impose new terms on current 
19 
AEP Ohio data center customers by reassigning them to this end-use rate schedule.  AEP 
20 
Ohio maintains that it is not proposing such a reassignment in this case, but its Application 
21 
 
10 AEP Ohio Response to OMAEG-INT-01-023, which is included in Exhibit KCH-2. 
11 AEP Ohio Response to DCC-INT-01-006d-h, which is included in Exhibit KCH-2.
