HIGGINS / 20 
attempts to “reserve the right” to do so in the future.12  The Company’s attempt to reserve 
1 
this “right” bears an uncomfortable resemblance to a “bait and switch” business practice in 
2 
which data center customers entered into contracts in good faith under one set of terms and 
3 
committed billions of dollars in capital funding to develop their facilities, only to be subject 
4 
after-the-fact to material changes to those terms on a discriminatory basis.  Whatever the 
5 
Commission determines with respect to the proposed new tariff, it should soundly reject 
6 
AEP Ohio’s attempt to reserve the right to switch current customers to the proposed new 
7 
rate. The Commission should not allow AEP Ohio to create such unwarranted uncertainty 
8 
for existing data center customers.  This problem can be avoided by rejecting the 
9 
Company’s proposal to adopt a special tariff that targets a specific business activity.  
10 
C.  The Minimum Demand Charge for Large Load Customers Should Remain at 
11 
60%. 
12 
Q. 
HOW DOES A MINIMUM DEMAND CHARGE WORK? 
13 
A. 
In the context of AEP Ohio’s tariff, a minimum demand charge requires that a customer 
14 
pay a monthly charge for demand that is no less than a stated percentage of its contract 
15 
demand, irrespective of its actual metered demand in that month.  Currently, General 
16 
Service (GS) customers are required to enter into one-year contracts that are subject to a 
17 
minimum billing demand of 60%.  So, for example, if a GS customer has a contract demand 
18 
of 10 MW, its minimum billing demand would be 6 MW.  If, in a given month, the customer 
19 
experiences a maximum metered demand of 8 MW, which exceeds its minimum billing 
20 
demand, its billing demand would be that same 8 MW for that month.  Alternatively, if the 
21 
customer’s maximum metered demand in a month was below its minimum billing demand, 
22 
 
12 Application at Paragraph 2. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 102 of 171
HIGGINS / 21 
say 4 MW, the customer would nonetheless be subject to the minimum billing demand of 
1 
6 MW.  The minimum demand charge provision therefore acts as a sort of “floor” for the 
2 
amount of revenue (via demand charges) AEP Ohio will recover from customers subject 
3 
to the provision. Under AEP’s proposed data center tariff, the minimum demand charge 
4 
for new data centers with contracted loads greater than 25 MW would be increased to 90% 
5 
of contract demand. 
6 
Q. 
IS AEP OHIO’S PROPOSAL TO INCREASE THE MINIMUM DEMAND 
7 
CHARGE FROM 60% TO 90% SUPPORTED BY CREDIBLE EVIDENCE?  
8 
A. 
No.  The Company provides no analysis or evidence to support an increase of any kind in 
9 
the minimum billing demand, let alone one of this magnitude, nor has it conducted any 
10 
analysis that supports the various contract terms it proposes, including the proposed ten-
11 
year contract term and exit fee provisions.13 
12 
Q. 
PLEASE ELABORATE ON THE COMPANY’S FAILURE TO SUPPORT ITS 
13 
MINIMUM DEMAND CHARGE PROPOSAL WITH ANALYSIS. 
14 
A. 
AEP Ohio has not conducted any quantitative analysis of the impact of anticipated data 
15 
center load on rates (for the industrial rate class or any other rate class), nor has it provided 
16 
any evaluation of the minimum demand charge, contract period, or other terms necessary 
17 
to ensure the Company recovers its forecast incremental revenue requirements over a 
18 
reasonable time frame without the risk of cost shifts or rate volatility.14 In fact, the 
19 
Company readily concedes its proposal is “not meant to perfectly align with investment 
20 
cost recovery.”15 Instead, the Company argues its proposal is intended to “provide potential 
21 
 
13 See AEP Ohio Responses to DCC-INT-01-007d, e; 008a, b; which are included in Exhibit KCH-2. 
14 See AEP Ohio Response to DCC-INT-01-007d, e, which are included in Exhibit KCH-2. 
15 Direct Testimony of Matthew M. McKenzie at 15. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 103 of 171
HIGGINS / 22 
data center customers substantial skin in the game”16 and suggests a 90% demand charge 
1 
would incentivize data center customers to request a more accurate capacity number.17 The 
2 
only quantitative analysis the Company offers is to calculate the increased revenue its 90% 
3 
minimum demand charge would provide from a hypothetical 500 MW customer.18  
4 
Q. 
WHAT IS YOUR REACTION TO AEP OHIO’S JUSTIFICATION FOR ITS 
5 
MINIMUM DEMAND CHARGE PROPOSAL? 
6 
A. 
As a threshold matter, it is self-evident that a 90% minimum demand charge will collect 
7 
more revenue than a 60% minimum demand charge.  But AEP Ohio fails to demonstrate 
8 
why a 90% minimum demand charge is the appropriate level, particularly if it is applied in 
9 
combination with the other features of its proposal, notably a long-term contract 
10 
requirement supported by collateral. By itself, requiring new large customers to enter into 
11 
long-term contracts supported by substantial capitalization or collateral ensures the “skin 
12 
in the game” AEP Ohio says it is seeking from these customers.  But AEP Ohio does not 
13 
even attempt to demonstrate that a 90% minimum demand charge is necessary to achieve 
14 
this objective.19 The Company’s proposed 90% minimum demand charge therefore appears 
15 
to be arbitrary. Establishing a reasonable and appropriate balance between contract term 
16 
and minimum demand charge requires more thoughtful analysis.  
17 
 
 
Regarding the minimum contract term, I agree that in light of the potential need for 
18 
transmission upgrades associated with significant load growth, it is reasonable to require 
19 
new large loads to enter into multi-year contracts to provide financial support for system 
20 
investments that may be needed to serve those loads.  To that end, I believe a minimum 
21 
 
16 Id., AEP Ohio Response to DCC-INT-01-012a, b, which are included in Exhibit KCH-2. 
17 Direct Testimony of Lisa O. Kelso at 10. 
18 Direct Testimony of Matthew M. McKenzie at 15-16.

HIGGINS / 23 
contract term of eight years is appropriate.  But the minimum demand charge should not 
1 
be increased to 90% in combination with this long-term contract requirement.  The current 
2 
60% minimum demand charge, in combination with a long-term contract should provide 
3 
sufficient assurance of revenue recovery from new large customer loads. 
4 
Q. 
WHY DOES THE CURRENT 60% MINIMUM DEMAND CHARGE, IN 
5 
COMBINATION WITH A LONG-TERM CONTRACT, PROVIDE SUFFICIENT 
6 
ASSURANCE OF REVENUE RECOVERY FROM NEW LARGE CUSTOMER 
7 
LOADS? 
8 
A. 
Fundamentally because, assuming no change to the current 60% minimum demand charge, 
9 
incremental revenues from new large customer load would comfortably cover the 
10 
incremental costs associated with the transmission infrastructure required to serve that load 
11 
over an eight-year contract term. I present an analysis of this issue in Exhibit KCH-1, which 
12 
examines the relationship between the recovery of minimum demand charges from new 
13 
large customers over a multi-year period and the potential long-term revenue requirements 
14 
associated with new transmission investment.   
15 
 
 
My analysis compares incremental long-term revenue requirements with 
16 
incremental long-term revenues via demand charges, holding all else equal. This is an 
17 
appropriate framework for analyzing the minimum demand charge because the central 
18 
issue raised by AEP Ohio in this case involves the possibility that the Company may have 
19 
to make long-term investments to serve new load, and in response to that possibility, AEP 
20 
Ohio is calling for long-term commitments from new large customers.  In this context, it is 
21 
useful to examine the question of revenue recovery from new large loads over a multi-year 
22 
period. 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 105 of 171
HIGGINS / 24 
Q. 
PLEASE DESCRIBE YOUR ANALYSIS IN MORE DETAIL, INCLUDING THE 
1 
ASSUMPTIONS UNDERLYING YOUR ANALYSIS AND THE RESULTS OF 
2 
THAT ANALYSIS. 
3 
A. 
My transmission cost assumptions are shown on page 1 of Exhibit KCH-1, which are based 
4 
on Midwestern Independent System Operator transmission cost and capacity estimates, and 
5 
which are also consistent with cost estimates provided by AEP Ohio.  The base case 
6 
assumptions in my analysis are presented in lines 1-6 on page 1, which assumes 
7 
construction of a 120-mile 765 kV transmission line (similar to the Vassel station to 
8 
Kammer station transmission line Company witness Ali describes in his testimony) at a 
9 
cost of $6,000,000 per mile.  The net present value of the 40-year revenue requirement for 
10 
such an investment is approximately $1.2 billion, as shown on line 8.  I also present an 
11 
alternative analysis that assumes a high-end cost of $10,000,000 per mile (lines 9-16) and 
12 
another that assumes a 200 mile line at $6,000,000 per mile (lines 17-24).  
13 
 
 
On page 2 of Exhibit KCH-1, I compare the net present value of the revenue 
14 
requirement that would be recovered from new large customers over eight years under the 
15 
current 60% minimum demand charge to that which would be recovered from them if they 
16 
were subjected to the 90% minimum demand charge AEP Ohio proposes.  In both cases, I 
17 
use AEP’s current Basic Transmission Charge Rider (“BTCR”) demand charge for GS 
18 
customers of $7.45/kW-month to calculate the revenues recovered from new large loads.  
19 
As shown in Exhibit KCH-1, page 2, line 27, a new large customer load that is subject to 
20 
the current BTCR charge at a 60% minimum billing demand would contribute at least 
21 
$53,640 per MW of contract demand each year to AEP Ohio’s transmission revenue 
22 
requirement.   
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 106 of 171
HIGGINS / 25 
 
 
For the purpose of this analysis I assume that projections of increased demand cause 
1 
AEP Ohio to pursue construction of a new 765 kV line through the PJM planning process.  
2 
As I noted above, a ballpark estimate to construct a line of this voltage is $6 million per 
3 
mile.20  If 120 miles of construction are required, e.g., the length of the Vassell to Kammer 
4 
line,21 the estimated capital costs would be $720 million.  Shown on page 1, line 6, over a 
5 
40-year life, this cost would correspond to an incremental revenue requirement of 
6 
approximately $1.2 billion on a present value basis before considering the allocation of 
7 
costs among PJM members.22  While transmission planning involves the complex interplay 
8 
of many variables, it is reasonable to expect a line of this size to accommodate around 
9 
6,625 MW of incremental transmission capacity.23 After adjusting for losses, this 
10 
corresponds to 6,407 MW of incremental load taking service at transmission voltage.24   
11 
 
 
If, hypothetically, this incremental transmission capacity accommodated 6,407 
12 
MW of new load from large customers subject to eight-year contracts with a 60% minimum 
13 
demand, the associated minimum revenue recovered from these customers would be $344 
14 
million per year, as shown on page 2, line 34.  Over eight years, the minimum revenue 
15 
recovered from these customers would have a net present value of $2.097 billion – 
16 
considerably greater than the 40-year net present value of the total expected incremental 
17 
revenue requirement from construction of the new line ($1.2 billion).25  Indeed, a 60% 
18 
minimum demand charge over eight years provides more than sufficient revenue coverage

17

18 
A. 
No, and therefore, the analysis I describe above is conservative from an AEP Ohio cost

Q. 
PLEASE ELABORATE. 
1 
A. 
Schedule 12 of the PJM Tariff specifies that 50% of the cost of AC facilities that operate 
2 
at or above 500 kV, as well as various other facilities that have the characteristics of 
3 
Regional Facilities, are allocated annually on a load ratio share basis across PJM based 
4 
upon each zone’s respective non-coincident peak load. Thus, half the cost of such a line 
5 
would be socialized across PJM, of which AEP Ohio represents approximately 6.24%.29  
6 
The remaining 50% of project cost is allocated based upon operational adequacy and 
7 
performance benefits received.  Of the portion allocated to AEP East based on benefit, the 
8 
share allocated to AEP Ohio would be based on AEP Ohio’s 12 Coincident Peak (“CP”) 
9 
demand relative to the 12 CP demands of the other AEP East utilities, which is currently 
10 
43.65%.30  Ultimately, depending on load growth in the AEP Ohio service territory relative 
11 
to that of other AEP East utilities, the cost share of a new 765 kV line borne by AEP Ohio 
12 
customers could very well be in the range of 28%-35% of the total revenue requirement.31 
13 
 
 
My demonstration of the robust cost coverage provided by long-term contracts with 
14 
a 60% minimum demand charge has been in the context of the total incremental revenue 
15 
requirement associated with a new 765 kV line.  In relation to the likely AEP Ohio 
16 
incremental revenue requirement (approximately 28%-35% of the total incremental 
17 
revenue requirement), the cost coverage provided by a 60% minimum demand charge 
18 
would also be robust.  As shown in Exhibit KCH-1, page 3, if AEP Ohio were to be 
19 
allocated 35% of the incremental revenue requirement associated with a new 765 kV 
20 
 
29 A recent PJM allocation of Regional Facilities indicates that AEP East’s load ratio share is 14.29%.  AEP Ohio’s

7 
Q. 
PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR RECOMMENDATIONS REGARDING THE 
8 
MINIMUM DEMAND CHARGE. 
9 
A. 
AEP Ohio’s proposal to increase the minimum demand charge for large data center 
10 
customers from 60% to 90% should be rejected.  Instead, the Commission should retain 
11 
the 60% minimum demand charge in combination with a long-term contract requirement 
12 
of eight years and apply this provision to all new large loads.  I have shown that if major 
13 
system upgrades are needed to accommodate future load growth, the combination of 60% 
14 
minimum demand charge with an eight-year contract provides more than sufficient revenue 
15 
requirement coverage under reasonable incremental cost scenarios.  A key implication 
16 
from my analysis is that while AEP Ohio may incur incremental costs to serve new large 
17 
loads, those same loads will bring incremental billing determinants and new revenues that 
18 
are not only expected to recover the incremental cost of the new investment, but will also 
19 
contribute to the recovery of the Company’s current (embedded) transmission costs, with 
20 
the potential to place downward pressure on rates over time. 
21 
 
32 Compare Exhibit KCH-1 page 3, line 58 to page 2, line 35.

HIGGINS / 29 
D.  The Company’s Proposed Tariff Language Regarding Involuntary 
1 
Curtailment Is Unduly Discriminatory and the Commission Should Reject 
2 
That Proposal. 
3 
Q. 
WHAT 
IS 
AEP 
OHIO 
PROPOSING 
REGARDING 
EMERGENCY 
4 
INTERRUPTION OF LARGE DATA CENTER LOADS? 
5 
A. 
AEP Ohio’s proposal is not clear. Its proposed tariff includes language stating that a large 
6 
data center customer is required to reduce its demand upon the Company’s system during 
7 
an RTO or Company-declared emergency event (including but not limited to applicable 
8 
NERC Energy Emergency Alert level declarations by PJM).33  In its Application, however, 
9 
AEP Ohio suggests something much broader, stating:  
10 
Both tariffs require that customers participate in the PJM Emergency [Load] 
11 
Response program or Company-declared emergency event and subject [sic] 
12 
to service disconnection if they do not respond.34  
13 
 
PJM’s Emergency Load Response program35 is designed to be voluntary and provides 
14 
economic incentives customers to participate.  New large loads in AEP Ohio’s service 
15 
territory should be empowered to consider participation in this program based on their 
16 
assessment of the benefits versus the costs, just like any other customers.  The Company’s 
17 
statement in its Application that participation in the PJM Emergency Load Response 
18 
program be mandatory should either be withdrawn or rejected. 
19 
 
 
In its Reply Comments, AEP Ohio disavows the plain reading of the language in 
20 
its Application, stating that: 
21 
Intervenors’ concerns are unfounded. AEP Ohio’s proposed tariff language 
22 
does not mandate that data center customers participate in any voluntary 
23 
programs where customers are compensated for curtailing load, such as AEP 
24 
 
33 Exhibit MSM-1, p. 5. 
34 Application at Paragraph 31. 
35 PJM OATT, Attachment K – Appendix, Section 8.1. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 111 of 171
HIGGINS / 30 
Ohio’s Interruptible Power Rider (“IRP”) or PJM’s demand response 
1 
program. Rather, the tariff language states that data centers could be required 
2 
to involuntarily curtail their load if there were an extreme energy emergency 
3 
on the grid. AEP Ohio would only require this involuntary curtailment in a 
4 
situation where voluntary demand response programs and other PJM or AEP 
5 
Ohio initiatives have not resolved the problem, and involuntary curtailments 
6 
are necessary to maintain the stability of the grid.  
7 
 
The source of confusion as to the Company’s intention regarding the curtailment of data 
8 
center load lies with AEP Ohio’s contradictory statements, suggesting that this provision 
9 
is not well-thought out.  AEP Ohio offers no testimony explaining this provision nor does 
10 
the Company explain why data centers in particular should be targeted for involuntary 
11 
curtailment during certain system emergencies.  Certainly, there are instances when 
12 
emergency conditions require load shedding to preserve the safe and stable operation of 
13 
the grid.  If voluntary demand response programs are insufficient to resolve a particular 
14 
event, then I agree that involuntary curtailment is a regrettable next step.  However, I do 
15 
not agree that it is reasonable to selectively “draft” a particular industry, such as data 
16 
centers, to be the first line of defense when involuntary curtailments are required. Such a 
17 
proposal is arbitrary and unduly discriminatory.  Mandatory curtailment could force data 
18 
centers to take customers offline and create harmful impacts for the increasing number of 
19 
industries that rely on them, including healthcare, financial services, transportation, e-
20 
commerce and many other sectors that depend on data centers for real-time services.   
21 
Q. 
WHAT DO AEP OHIO’S CURRENTLY-EFFECTIVE TARIFFS REQUIRE WITH 
22 
RESPECT TO INVOLUNTARY CURTAILMENT? 
23 
A. 
AEP Ohio’s tariff already includes the following provision regarding service interruptions: 
24 
The Company does not guarantee continuous regular and uninterrupted 
25 
supply of service.  The Company may, without liability, interrupt or limit the 
26 
supply of service for the purpose of making repairs, changes, or 
27 
improvements in any part of its system for the general good of the service or 
28 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 112 of 171
HIGGINS / 31 
the safety of the public or for the purpose of preventing or limiting any actual 
1 
or threatened instability or disturbance of the system.36 
2 
 
Thus, the tariff already permits AEP Ohio to curtail service to any customer to prevent or 
3 
limit any instability or system disturbance.  AEP Ohio’s involuntary curtailment protocol 
4 
should be implemented on a non-discriminatory basis with respect to business activity, 
5 
except for those customers, such as hospitals, that require special protection against 
6 
interruption as a matter of the public interest.  The Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s 
7 
data center-specific involuntary curtailment provision.  
8 
Q. 
DO YOU HAVE ANY COMMENTS ON AEP’S PROPOSED TARIFF LANGUAGE 
9 
REGARDING 
CUSTOMERS’ 
ADHERENCE 
TO 
ALL 
APPLICABLE 
10 
TECHNICAL REQUIREMENTS? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  AEP Ohio includes language in its proposed data center tariff that requires customers 
12 
to follow all applicable technical operating requirements, such as not intentionally or 
13 
unintentionally cycling load in a way that creates an imbalanced or unacceptable system 
14 
frequency.  I have no objection to such a requirement, but adhering to all applicable 
15 
technical operating requirements more properly belongs in the Company’s tariff as a 
16 
general requirement, not one that is specific to an individual rate schedule.  As such, I 
17 
recommend that the Commission exclude such a provision from any rate schedule or rate 
18 
schedule modifications adopted in this case and instead direct AEP Ohio to relocate this 
19 
provision to a section of the tariff of more general applicability.  
20 
 
36 AEP Ohio Tariff, Section 31.17. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 113 of 171
HIGGINS / 32 
E.  
AEP Ohio’s Proposal to Restrict the Load Ramping Period to Three Years for 
1 
Data Centers Greater than 25 MW Should Be Rejected as Impractical and 
2 
Unreasonably Restrictive. 
3 
Q. 
WHAT IS AEP OHIO PROPOSING WITH RESPECT TO THE LOAD RAMPING 
4 
PERIOD FOR NEW LARGE DATA CENTERS? 
5 
A. 
AEP Ohio proposes that new large data centers be obligated to pay the minimum demand 
6 
charge for their ultimately planned maximum contract load within three years of taking 
7 
service.  Within the first three years, the data center would be permitted to phase in its 
8 
contract demand obligations.  This phase-in is referred to as the “load ramp.”37   
9 
Q. 
WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE TO THE COMPANY’S LOAD RAMPING 
10 
PROPOSAL? 
11 
A. 
With the exception of setting the minimum demand at 90% (discussed above), AEP Ohio’s 
12 
proposed treatment during the first three years of the load ramp is reasonable.  However, 
13 
limiting the load ramp to only three years is not reasonable.  Data centers are scalable 
14 
facilities and imposing a rigid three-year ramp schedule is impractical and unrealistic. Data 
15 
centers, with their diverse purposes – such as cloud computing, AI, or hybrid models – 
16 
require tailored ramping schedules.  The ramps will not and should not all be the same.  
17 
Instead, the customer should be able to set the ramping schedule based on its facts and 
18 
circumstances and in communication with the utility.  There are no efficiencies gained with 
19 
this proposed restriction, in fact, quite the opposite.  
20 
 
 
Should the timeline for capacity availability be a concern, transparent commitment 
21 
to and accounting of a customer’s load ramp will enable efficient system build-out. In 
22 
 
37 Direct Testimony of Matthew M. McKenzie at 27. 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 114 of 171
HIGGINS / 33 
contrast, AEP Ohio’s proposed requirement will result in a false urgency for additional 
1 
investment by artificially accelerating ramp rates for some customers.  I recommend that 
2 
the customer be allowed to commit to a load ramp schedule that suits their business and 
3 
that no restrictions be placed on the schedule. 
4 
F.  
It Is Reasonable for New Large Loads to be Required to Sign Long-Term 
5 
Contracts with Minimum Demand Provisions and an Associated Exit Fee, but 
6 
with Modifications to AEP Ohio’s Proposal. 
7 
Q. 
WHAT DOES AEP OHIO PROPOSE WITH RESPECT TO CONTRACT TERMS 
8 
AND EXIT FEES? 
9 
A. 
As I noted above, AEP Ohio proposes mandatory ten-year contracts with an exit provision 
10 
after five years and an associated exit fee equal to three years of minimum contract demand 
11 
payments.  Effectively, this results in an eight-year minimum commitment.   
12 
Q. 
PLEASE EXPLAIN YOUR PROPOSED MODIFICATIONS TO AEP OHIO’S 
13 
PROPOSED LONG-TERM CONTRACT REQUIREMENTS AND EXIT FEE. 
14 
A. 
I recommend the minimum term for new large loads be set at eight years to reflect the 
15 
effective minimum term commitment in AEP’s proposal, although I support customers 
16 
having the option to enter into a longer term contract on a voluntary basis. 
17 
 
 
With respect to the exit fee, given the substantial potential for load growth in the 
18 
AEP Ohio service territory as described by AEP Ohio, and the large minimum demand 
19 
commitment associated with eight-year contracts, it would be reasonable to implement a 
20 
process whereby any capacity that is relinquished by an exiting customer is made available 
21 
to serve requests for additional load corresponding to the time period for which capacity is 
22 
relinquished.  Capacity that is relinquished by an exiting customer (or by one reducing its 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 115 of 171
HIGGINS / 34 
capacity), but which can effectively be “transferred” to serve new load, should not be 
1 
subject to the full exit fee. 
2 
Q. 
WHY IS IT REASONABLE FOR EXIT FEES TO BE OFFSET BY CAPACITY 
3 
THAT CAN BE UTILIZED BY NEW LOAD? 
4 
A. 
Moving from one-year contracts to contracts with minimum demand charges and eight year 
5 
terms places substantial business risk on customers, whose business plans and need for 
6 
power may change over a period that long.  Exit fees are appropriate when they are needed 
7 
to protect other customers from the consequences of a revenue shortfall which could occur 
8 
if a large customer exits the system prior to the end of its contract term.  However, if the 
9 
capacity the exiting customer is relinquishing can be usefully deployed to serve a new load 
10 
over the same time period, then the mitigation provided by the exit fee would not be 
11 
necessary.  This is particularly true if future customers are subject to long lead times to 
12 
obtain service for new load.  Relinquished capacity could be a source of capacity that might 
13 
not otherwise be available to serve new load.   
14 
 
 
The upshot is that exit fees provide a kind of insurance for the other customers on 
15 
the system, but if the departure of a customer’s load does not impose a revenue shortfall 
16 
on the system because the capacity can be redeployed, then it is reasonable to waive the 
17 
charge to the extent the capacity can be used by others.  I recommend that the Commission 
18 
order AEP Ohio to implement a process for transferring relinquished capacity in 
19 
conjunction with requiring long-term contracts with minimum demand charges. 
20 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 116 of 171
HIGGINS / 35 
Q. 
SHOULD THERE BE ANY OTHER CONSIDERATIONS IN DETERMINING A 
1 
TERMINATING CUSTOMER’S EXIT FEE? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  For any month in which a terminating customer had paid demand charges in excess 
3 
of its minimum billing demand, it would be appropriate to credit that excess against the 
4 
remaining revenue obligation in calculating the exit fee.  The exit fee appropriately focuses 
5 
on recovering revenues associated with the minimum demand charge.  If the customer had 
6 
previously made payments in excess of this amount, it would be reasonable to credit the 
7 
excess portion of such prior payments against the exit fee owing.  In addition, the tariff 
8 
should permit exiting customers to negotiate mutually agreeable exit terms with AEP Ohio, 
9 
which would allow both parties to take into account factors and considerations not foreseen 
10 
at this time.  
11 
G.  It is Reasonable to Adopt Enhanced Collateral Requirements for New Large 
12 
Loads on a Non-Discriminatory Basis as to Business Activity.  
13 
Q. 
WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE TO AEP OHIO’S PROPOSED CHANGES TO 
14 
SECURITY AND COLLATERAL PROVISIONS FOR NEW LARGE LOADS?  
15 
A. 
I recognize the need for enhanced security and collateral provisions for new large loads 
16 
during a time of load growth that may require infrastructure expansion.  As such, I do not 
17 
object to the Company’s proposed collateral requirements requiring—for certain customers  
18 
lacking sufficiently robust credit or sufficient liquidity—financial support for 50% of the 
19 
minimum charges over the term of the contract in the context of the eight-year contract 
20 
term and 60% minimum demand charge I am recommending in this case, so long as these 
21 
requirements are applied to all new large loads on a non-discriminatory basis, i.e., the 
22 
provisions do not discriminate against data center customers.  For a longer term or higher 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 117 of 171
HIGGINS / 36 
minimum demand charge, I recommend that the 50% financial coverage be scaled back 
1 
proportionately. 
2 
Q. 
WHAT OPTIONS DOES AEP OHIO PROPOSE FOR MEETING THE 
3 
COLLATERAL REQUIREMENTS? 
4 
A. 
AEP Ohio proposes several options whereby these requirements can be met: (1) a guarantee 
5 
by the corporate parent or an affiliate with sufficient credit metrics; (2) a standby letter of 
6 
credit; or (3) cash.  These options strike me as generally reasonable.  In addition, the 
7 
amount of the collateral requirement would be reduced by one year’s minimum charges for 
8 
each year the customer is energized and makes on-time payments under the contract, 
9 
another provision that I support. 
10 
H.  The Company’s Responsibilities to Customers Signing Long-Term Contracts 
11 
Requires Greater Specificity. 
12 
Q. 
UNDER THE PROPOSED DATA CENTER TARIFF, DOES AEP OHIO MAKE 
13 
ANY COMMITMENTS WITH RESPECT TO THE TIMELY DELIVERY OF 
14 
CONTRACT CAPACITY? 
15 
A. 
No.  Under the Company’s proposal, large data center customers would be required to meet 
16 
certain capitalization thresholds or collateralize 50% of the minimum demand charges 
17 
associated with a ten-year contract, but are offered no assurance that the product they are 
18 
committing to purchase will be made available to them in a timely manner.  It is a decidedly 
19 
one-sided arrangement. 
20 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 118 of 171
HIGGINS / 37 
Q. 
WHAT RESPONSIBILITIES TO THE CUSTOMERS SIGNING LONG-TERM 
1 
SHOULD BE BETTER SPELLED OUT IN THE TARIFF? 
2 
A. 
The Company should commit to delivery of contract capacity on the schedule designated 
3 
in the electric service agreement.  In the event that any delays are anticipated, the Company 
4 
should be required to notify the customer as soon as practicable and initiate a consultative 
5 
process to develop a remediation plan or to provide temporary power delivery solutions.  
6 
Remedial actions taken in response to a delay that are not caused by the customer should 
7 
not result in additional charges.  Further, in the event of delay, the term to which minimum 
8 
demand charges would apply should be reduced commensurate with the delay.  Finally, if 
9 
AEP Ohio is unable after one year to meet the delivery date in the contract and no remedial 
10 
actions have been taken to mitigate the problem on a temporary basis, the customer should 
11 
be permitted to terminate its contract without penalty. 
12 
 
13 
VI.  
SUMMARY 
14 
Q. 
PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR RECOMMENDATIONS. 
15 
A. 
I offer the following recommendations: 
16 
 
 
1.  It is reasonable for the Commission to require new large loads of 50 MW or 
17 
greater to sign long-term contracts with minimum demand provisions and an associated 
18 
exit fee supported either by substantial capitalization or collateral.  Specifically, I 
19 
recommend the Commission adopt a mandatory minimum contract term for new large 
20 
loads of eight years, with a 24-month exit notice requirement and an exit fee that is based 
21 
on the foregone net revenues from the minimum demand charge over the remaining 
22 
contract term after accounting for the release or transfer of the terminated capacity to serve 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 119 of 171
HIGGINS / 38 
new load.  In addition, the tariff should permit exiting customers to negotiate mutually 
1 
agreeable exit terms with AEP Ohio. 
2 
 
 
2.  The Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s proposal to structure a tariff change 
3 
based on the business activity of the customer (i.e. data centers) rather than the 
4 
characteristics of the customer’s load.  Instead, the changes to the tariff terms under 
5 
consideration in this docket should be applicable to any new large loads of 50 MW or 
6 
greater on a non-discriminatory basis as to end use.   
7 
 
 
3.  The Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s attempt to “reserve the right” to 
8 
migrate data center customers with signed electric service agreements to the proposed new 
9 
tariff in a later rate proceeding.  
10 
 
 
4.  The Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s proposal to increase the minimum 
11 
billing demand from 60% to 90% for data center customers as it lacks any analytical 
12 
support in the Company’s filing.  
13 
 
 
5.  It is reasonable for AEP Ohio to require that certain new large loads not meeting 
14 
specified capitalization requirements provide collateral to support 50% of the minimum 
15 
billing demand for eight years with a 60% minimum demand charge. For a longer term or 
16 
higher minimum demand charge, I recommend that the Commission scale back the 50% 
17 
financial coverage requirement proportionately. 
18 
 
 
6.  AEP Ohio’s proposal that new data center loads greater than 25 MW be subject 
19 
to emergency interruption is poorly articulated and the Company provides no justification 
20 
for singling out data centers for such interruptions.  The Commission should therefore 
21 
reject this proposed provision.  In addition, AEP Ohio’s proposed language requiring 
22 
customers to follow all applicable technical requirements should not be included in any 
23 
Supp. Appx. Page Page 120 of 171
HIGGINS / 39 
rate schedule changes approved in that case, but instead be relocated to a section of the 
1 
tariff of more general applicability. 
2 
 
 
7.  The Commission should reject AEP Ohio’s proposal to restrict the load ramping 
3 
period for data centers greater than 25 MW to three years because it is both impractical and 
4 
unreasonably restrictive.   
5 
 
 
8.  AEP Ohio should commit to delivery of contract capacity on the schedule 
6 
designated in the electric service agreement.  In the event that AEP Ohio is unable to meet 
7 
the contracted delivery date, remedies should be made available to the customer in the 
8 
tariff.  
9 
Q. 
HOW 
SHOULD 
THE 
COMMISSION 
IMPLEMENT 
YOUR 
10 
RECOMMENDATIONS? 
11 
A. 
My recommendations can be fully implemented by modifying Schedule GS to add 
12 
additional requirements for customers with new loads with contract demands of 50 MW or 
13 
greater. I offer proposed modifications to Schedule GS reflecting the recommendations I 
14 
make in my testimony as Exhibit KCH-3. 
15 
Q. 
DOES THIS CONCLUDE YOUR DIRECT TESTIMONY? 
16 
A. 
Yes. 
17

DCC-INT-01-005
On page 10 of Lisa Kelso’s direct testimony filed on May 13, 2024 in

(over 25 MW) minimum contract capacity from 60% to 90% will also 
encourage data center customers to request a more accurate capacity 
(MW) number.”

a more accurate capacity number? 
b.: Please explain in detail why AEP believes that a minimum contract 
capacity of 60% is insufficient to incentivize data center customers to 
request an accurate level of contract capacity? 
c.: Please explain in detail why AEP believes that a minimum contract 
capacity of 60% is sufficient to incentivize large non-data center 
customers to request an accurate level of contract capacity? 
d.: Please explain in detail whether AEP believes that a minimum 
contract capacity of 70% would be sufficient to incentivize data center 
customers to request an accurate level of contract capacity? If not, 
please explain why not.

contract capacity of 80% would be sufficient to incentivize data center 
customers to request an accurate level of contract capacity? If not,

customer to serve other customers in the queue? Please explain in 
detail why or why not.

capacity to serve other customers, does AEP believe that the proposed 
minimum contract capacity proposal will result in an over-recovery of 
revenues? Please explain in detail why or why not.

a. As described throughout AEP Ohio’s application and testimony, the purpose of AEP 
Ohio’s proposed tariffs is, in part, to provide data centers greater incentives to accurately 
forecast the amount of load that they sign up for in an ESA. 
b. AEP Ohio objects to this request insofar as it requires AEP Ohio to provided an 
exhaustive list of reasons.  AEP Ohio further objects to this request as vague and 
ambiguous; AEP Ohio specifically objects to the phrase “in detail” as vague and 
undefined.  The reasons for AEP Ohio’s proposals are provided throughout AEP Ohio’s 
Application and testimony.  Subject to these objections and without waiving them, AEP 
Ohio responds that the 60% minimum is insufficient because it does not provide enough 
financial incentive for data center customers to accurately forecast their actual needs in 
their requested contract capacity in an ESA.  As AEP Ohio witness McKenzie explains 
(at 16), the difference between a 60% minimum and 100% contract capacity would be 
$26.8M annually. 
c. As explained in AEP Ohio witness McKenzie’s testimony, AEP Ohio is proposing to 
apply a 90% minimum (and other proposed tariff provisions) to data center but not to 
other large customers for multiple reasons.  For one thing, “the recent, massive load 
growth in AEP Ohio’s service territory has been drive primarily by data centers, not 
other large loads.”  (McKenzie Testimony at 7-8.)  Also, “historically AEP Ohio’s 
largest customers have been large industrial facilities whose peak power demand was in 
the range of a few hundred MW.  Now AEP Ohio has had multiple customers express 
interest in building data centers that plan to reach loads of 1,000 MW or more.”  (Id. at 
8.)  Furthermore, “[d]ata centers are also easily scalable in a way that large industrial 
facilities are not. As a result, it is possible that the load figures we are seeing today are 
just the beginning, and data center facilities could grow even larger in the future.  AEP 
Ohio’s current tariffs were not designed with these large loads in mind, and there are 
limitations in the current tariffs that AEP Ohio proposes to address with data-center-
specific tariffs.”  (Id.) 
d. AEP Ohio objects to this request insofar as it requires AEP Ohio to provided an 
exhaustive list of reasons.  AEP Ohio further objects to this request as vague and 
ambiguous; AEP Ohio specifically objects to the phrase “in detail” as vague and 
undefined.  The reasons for AEP Ohio’s proposals are provided throughout AEP Ohio’s

h.: Did AEP conduct an economic analysis to justify this change in 
minimum demand charges for data centers?
 
RESPONSE 
 
The Company objects to the form of the question as this request is vague, overbroad and/or 
unduly burdensome.  Without waiving the foregoing objection(s) or any general objection the 
Company may have, the Company states as follows.  The Company objects to the 
characterization of testimony as being inaccurate.  Without waiving the foregoing objection(s) 
or any general objection the Company may have, the Company states as follows. The Company 
objects because it is unable to fully answer the hypothetical question posed in the absence of all 
of the pertinent assumptions and fact/circumstances that apply to the hypothetical 
scenario.  Without waiving the foregoing objection(s) or any general objection the Company 
may have, the Company states as follows. 
a-c.  AEP Ohio objects to the requests on the ground that what alternative regulatory proposals 
AEP Ohio considered in preparation for this proceeding is protected by attorney-client privilege 
and the attorney work product doctrine. The reasons and explanations supporting the proposals 
in the application are thoroughly addressed in the Company’s testimonies. 
d-h.  As explained in AEP Ohio witness McKenzie’s testimony (at 4), the purpose of AEP 
Ohio’s proposal is, in part, to keep AEP Ohio’s service territory open for economic 
development by requiring new data center customers to make long-term financial commitments 
that will support transmission investment to facilitate both the growth of ordinary job-creating 
economic development and the growth of data centers.  Further explanation of how AEP Ohio’s 
proposed data center tariff promotes economic development is provided in the testimony of 
AEP Ohio witness Kelso (at 8-10).  AEP Ohio has not conducted any formal economic analysis 
comparing the possible scenario in which the proposed data center tariff is approved versus any 
other scenario, such as the data center tariff being approved with modifications or denied.

INTERROGATORY 
 
DCC-INT-01-007 
On page 17 of Matthew McKenzie’s direct testimony filed on May 13, 
2024 in this proceeding, he states, “AEP Ohio’s proposed Schedule 
DCP provides that AEP Ohio and data center customers must enter 
into “contracts” – meaning ESAs – with an initial term of ten years” 
and “AEP Ohio’s current general service tariffs contemplate one-year 
electric service contracts.” 
 
a.: What specific factors or considerations led to the decision to 
mandate 10-year initial terms for ESAs under the proposed Schedule 
DCP? 
 
b.: Has AEP conducted any analysis to anticipate how the 10-year 
initial term requirement could impact the operational planning and 
financial flexibility of data center customers? If so, please provide this 
analysis. 
 
c.: Were any alternative contract term lengths considered for data 
centers? If so, please describe these alternatives and the rationale for 
ultimately selecting a 10-year term. 
 
d.: Has AEP conducted any analysis on the expected revenue impact 
on AEP resulting from the change in contract terms from one year to 
10 years for data centers? If so, please provide that analysis. 
 
e.: Did AEP prepare an economic analysis to justify this change in the 
ESA contract term length to 10 years for data centers? 
 
f.: Has AEP assessed any potential negative impacts on economic 
development or data center attraction/retention in the region due to the 
10-year initial term requirement? If so, please provide the findings of 
such assessments. 
 
g.: Has AEP evaluated the potential impact of the 10-year initial term 
requirement on construction jobs associated with data center projects? 
If so, please provide the findings and underlying analysis of such 
evaluations. 
 
h.: Has AEP considered the potential effects of the 10-year initial term 
requirement on tax revenue generated by data center projects? If so, 
please provide the findings and underlying analysis of such 
evaluations.

RESPONSE 
 
The Company objects to the form of the question as this request is vague, overbroad and/or 
unduly burdensome.  Without waiving the foregoing objection(s) or any general objection the 
Company may have, the Company states as follows.  The Company objects to the 
characterization of testimony as being inaccurate.  Without waiving the foregoing objection(s) 
or any general objection the Company may have, the Company states as follows. The Company 
objects because it is unable to fully answer the hypothetical question posed in the absence of all 
of the pertinent assumptions and fact/circumstances that apply to the hypothetical 
scenario.  Without waiving the foregoing objection(s) or any general objection the Company 
may have, the Company states as follows. 
a. The specific factors and considerations supporting AEP Ohio’s proposal are described 
throughout AEP Ohio’s application and testimony.  An explanation of the proposed ten-
year ESA term is provided, among other places, in AEP Ohio witness McKenzie’s 
testimony (at 17-18). 
b. As explained in AEP Ohio witness McKenzie’s testimony (at 10), one purpose of the 
proposed tariffs is to facilitate data center growth in a rational and prudent way and “to 
break the logjam and provide a reasonable path forward to build infrastructure to serve 
data centers backed by long-term financial commitments that mitigate the risks to AEP 
Ohio and its customers.”  AEP Ohio has not conducted any formal “analysis” of 
“operational planning and financial flexibility of data center customers” comparing the 
possible scenario in which the proposed data center tariff is approved versus any other 
scenario, such as the data center tariff being approved with modifications or denied. 
c. AEP Ohio objects to the requests on the ground that what alternative regulatory 
proposals AEP Ohio considered in preparation for this proceeding is protected by 
attorney-client privilege and the attorney work product doctrine. 
d. As explained by AEP Ohio witness McKenzie, one purpose of AEP Ohio’s proposed 
tariffs is to ensure a source of revenue even if the data centers are never built or are built 
but do not use as much power as they originally asked for.  AEP Ohio has not conducted 
any formal “analysis” of “the expected revenue impact on AEP” comparing the possible 
scenario in which the proposed data center tariff is approved versus any other scenario, 
such as the data center tariff being approved with modifications or denied.  Such an 
analysis would depend, in part, in how many customers in AEP Ohio’s current queue of 
30,000 MW of potential data center customers would sign an ESA under the data center 
tariff as approved, which cannot be predicted at this time. The purpose of the Companies

proposed tariffs was to proactively work to protect existing and future of AEP Ohio

e-h. AEP Ohio has not conducted any formal economic analysis comparing the possible 
scenario in which the proposed data center tariff is approved versus any other scenario, such as 
the data center tariff being approved with modifications or denied. Please see AEP Ohio’s 
response to DCC-INT-01-006, Part (d). 
i.  By signing an ESA under AEP Ohio’s proposed tariff, a data center customer will be signing 
up for electric service at the requested MW once new EHV transmission and other potential 
investments are made that allow AEP Ohio to serve the customer.  In this proceeding, AEP 
Ohio is not proposing any additional “incentives” or “benefits” to data center customers beyond 
what is proposed in AEP Ohio’s application and testimony.

DCC-INT-01-008 
On page 20 of Matthew McKenzie’s direct testimony filed on May 13, 
2024 in this proceeding, he states “the proposed tariff provides that a 
customer may cancel the ESA after five years if the customer pays an 
“exit fee” equal to three years of minimum charges.” 
 
a.: How did AEP determine that the exit fee should consist of three 
years of minimum charges? 
 
b.: Did AEP conduct an economic analysis to justify the amount of the 
exit fee required by the tariff? 
 
c.: How do exit fees required by the tariff that are paid to AEP 
facilitate investment in transmission infrastructure? 
 
d.: Please explain in detail the regulatory accounting treatment that 
will be applied to any exit fees received by AEP. 
 
e.: If transmission capacity is built to serve forecasted load for a data 
center customer that pays an exit fee, and that transmission capacity is 
subsequently utilized to serve other customers’ loads, does AEP plan 
to return any portion of the exit fee?
 
RESPONSE 
 
The Company objects to the form of the question as this request is vague, overbroad and/or 
unduly burdensome.  Without waiving the foregoing objection(s) or any general objection the 
Company may have, the Company states as follows.  The Company objects to the 
characterization of testimony as being inaccurate.  Without waiving the foregoing objection(s)

objects because it is unable to fully answer the hypothetical question posed in the absence of all 
of the pertinent assumptions and fact/circumstances that apply to the hypothetical 
scenario.  Without waiving the foregoing objection(s) or any general objection the Company 
may have, the Company states as follows. 
a. As explained in AEP Ohio witness McKenzie’s testimony (at 18), the proposed “exit 
fee” was set at three years’ minimum charges to give data center customers “a small 
amount of relief” if their “plans drastically change” and they can “no longer go through 
with their commitments, while simultaneously protecting the rest of AEP Ohio’s 
customers.”  
b. Please see AEP Ohio’s response to DCC-INT-01-006, Part (d). 
c. As explained throughout AEP Ohio’s application and testimony, AEP Ohio’s proposed 
tariffs will facilitate transmission planning by giving data center customers greater

meant to provide potential data center customers substantial skin in the 
game.” Please explain in detail any corresponding actions AEP is 
taking to meet its reciprocal obligation to serve and guarantee capacity 
and timelines to data center customers who will take on the additional 
risks and obligations proposed in this tariff.

INTERROGATORY 
OEG-INT-1-3. 
Please provide a quantification of the billions of dollars to construct a 
new 120 mile 765 kV line referenced by Mr. McKenzie. In your 
quantification, please provide the cost of steel towers (including 
foundations) and wires, indicate how construction period interest was 
treated and identify the amount of interest, identify necessary substation 
upgrades and the cost, identify new substation costs, identify land 
acquisition, right of way and permitting costs, identify A&G and 
overhead costs, and identify whether a contingency was included and if 
so what percent.
RESPONSE 
AEP Ohio objects to the extent the request mischaracterizes the Company’s Application and 
prefiled Direct Testimony in this matter.  The Company further objects to this request as vague 
and undefined.  The Company further objects that the request calls for a narrative response. The 
Company further objects that the request is unduly burdensome because it is seeking information 
that is not kept in the ordinary course of business and would be unduly burdensome to 
provide.  Without waiving these objections or any general objections the Company may have, the 
Company states as follows.  The requested quantification has not been completed and is not 
available as requested. The Company has provided cost estimates on a general per-mile cost 
basis based on previous history and experience in constructing 765 kV transmission lines. 
Currently, new 765 kV lines are expected to cost $4-$7M per mile (including towers, conductors, 
associated hardware and right-of-way). This cost does not include any station or terminal costs. 
Additionally, right-of-way costs can vary significantly across the length of the line and all costs 
are subject to change over time. All costs will go through an engineering and estimating stage 
when solutions are identified and formalized; this illustration was provided to point to potential 
solutions and costs that would be associated with them. 
Prepared by:  
Counsel 
Kamran Ali

SCHEDULE GS 
(General Service) 
Definitions 
For purposes of this schedule: 
“Contract Capacity” means the customer-specified amount of monthly peak load requirements for 
each month as set forth in the contract for service. 
“Load Ramp Contract Capacity” means the mutually-agreed monthly peak load requirements 
associated with the Load Ramp Period. 
“Load Ramp Period” refers to the mutually-agreed period between commencement of service until 
the customer agrees to full Contract Capacity. 
“Minimum Demand Charges” refers to the demand charges assessed on a customer’s monthly 
billing demand. 
Availability of Service 
Available for general service to non-residential customers served under secondary, primary and 
transmission voltage and who request and receive electric generation service from either SSO or a qualified 
CRES Provider.

Minimum Charges 
The minimum monthly charge under this schedule shall be the sum of the customer charge, the product 
of the demand charge and the monthly billing demand, and all Commission-approved riders shown on Sheet 
Number 104-1. 
 
Monthly Billing Demand 
Billing demand in KW shall be taken each month as the single highest 30-minute integrated peak in KW 
as registered during the month by a 30-minute integrating demand meter or indicator, or at the 
Company's option, as the highest registration of a thermal-type demand meter. For accounts over 100 KW, 
monthly billing demand established hereunder shall not be less than 60% of the greater of (a) the customer's 
contract capacity in excess of 100 KW, or (b) the customer's highest previously established

SCHEDULE GS 
(General Service) 
monthly billing demand during the past 11 months in excess of 100 KW. In no event shall the monthly 
billing demand be less than the minimum billing demand, if any, specified in the service contract. 
 
If more than 50% of the customer's connected load is for electric space heating purposes, the minimum 
monthly billing demand for the billing months of June through October will be 25% of the greater of (a) the 
customer's contract capacity in excess of 100 KW, or (b) the customer's highest previously established 
monthly billing demand during the past 11 months in excess of 100 KW. 
 
For customers primarily engaged in seasonal agricultural related activities, the minimum monthly billing 
demand will be 25% of the greater of (a) the customer's contract capacity in excess of 100 KW, or (b) the 
customer's highest previously established monthly billing demand during the past 11 months in excess of 
100 KW. 
 
 
The Metered Voltage adjustment, as set forth below, shall not apply to the customer's minimum monthly 
billing demand. 
Excess KVA Demand 
 
The monthly KVA demand shall be determined by dividing the maximum metered KW demand by the 
average monthly power factor. 
The excess KVA demand, if any shall be the amount by which the monthly KVA demand exceeds the greater 
of (a) 115% of the maximum metered KW demand or (b) 100 KVA. 
 
The Metered Voltage adjustment, as set forth below, shall apply to the customer's excess KVA demand. 
Upon request, by a secondary or primary voltage customer having operational characteristics not aligned 
with its average monthly power factor, the Company will apply the Excess KVAR provision methodology that 
is applied to transmission voltage customers. The cost of any meter modifications required to accommodate 
such request shall be paid for by the customer. 
 
Optional Time-of-Day Provision for Determining the Transmission Component of the Monthly Bill 
 
Available to customers who operate primarily during the off-peak period (as set forth below) and request the 
installation of time-of-day metering in order to receive service under this provision. The customer shall be 
required to pay the necessary additional metering cost. 
For the purpose of this provision, the monthly billing demand as defined above shall be determined during 
the on-peak period. The off-peak excess demand shall be the amount by which the demand created during 
the off-peak period exceeds the monthly billing demand. 
 
For the purpose of this provision, the on-peak billing period is defined as 7 a.m. to 9 p.m. local time for all 
weekdays, Monday through Friday. The off-peak billing period is defined as 9 p.m. to 7 a.m. for all

SCHEDULE GS 
(General Service) 
weekdays, all hours of the day on Saturdays and Sundays, and the legal holidays of New Year's Day, 
Presidents' Day, Memorial Day, Independence Day, Labor Day, Thanksgiving Day and Christmas Day. 
 
Metered Voltage Adjustment 
The rates set forth in this schedule are based upon the delivery and measurement of energy at the same 
voltage, thus measurement will be made at or compensated to the delivery voltage. At the sole discretion of 
the Company such compensation may be achieved through the use of loss compensating equipment, the 
use of formulas to calculate losses or the application of multipliers to the metered quantities. In such cases 
the metered KWH, KW, kVAR and RKVAH values will be adjusted for billing purposes. If the Company elects 
to adjust KWH, KW, kVAR and RKVAH based on multipliers, the adjustment shall be in accordance with the 
following: 
(a) 
Measurements taken at the low-side of a customer-owned transformer will be multiplied 
by 1.01. 
(b) 
Measurements taken at the high-side of a Company-owned transformer will be multiplied 
by 0.98. 
Delayed Payment Charge 
Bills are due and payable in full by mail, checkless payment plan, electronic payment plan or at an authorized 
payment agent of the Company within 21 days after the mailing of the bill. On all accounts not paid by the 
due date, an additional charge of 2.5% of the unpaid balance will be due. Federal, state, county, township 
and municipal governments and public school systems not served under special contract are subject to 
the Public Authority Delayed Payment provision, Supplement 21. 
Applicable Riders 
 
Monthly Charges computed under this schedule shall be adjusted in accordance with the Commission- 
approved riders on Sheet Number 104-1.

*Initial contracts are required when the criteria applies to a new service or a service expansion which requires the construction of 
additional local facilities. 
The initial contract period will be for at least 1 year. The Company may, at its option, require a longer 
initial term of contract. After the initial term, on-going contracts will be self-renewing and may be modified or 
cancelled by either party with 30 days’ written notice to the other of the intention to alter service under the 
terms of this schedule. Contracts may not be modified with respect to reserved capacity more than once in 
a 12 month period. 
 
The Company shall not be required to supply capacity in excess of that contracted for except by mutual 
agreement. 
State agencies will be exempt from any requirement for initial and on-going contracts but may enter into a 
contract when mutually agreed upon by both parties. When a contract is not pursued, the customer shall 
submit any new or expanded service requirements in writing to the company. These requirements shall 
include anticipated peak demand (to be used for minimum billing demand purposes), requested delivery 
voltage, a specification of the physical delivery point where the company’s facilities will meet the customer’s 
facilities and any unique or complex service configurations including but not limited to Alternate Feed 
Service, large motor or power electronic loads with risk of objectionable flicker/harmonics. 
 
Special Terms and Conditions 
This schedule is subject to the Company's Terms and Conditions of Service. 
 
Customers with cogeneration and/or small power production facilities which qualify under Section 210 of the 
Public Utility Regulatory Policies Act of 1978 shall take service under Schedule COGEN/SPP, Schedule 
NEMS, or by special agreement with the Company. All other customers having sources of electrical energy 
supply other than the Company shall take service under the applicable General Service Schedule or 
Schedule NEMS. 
At its discretion, the Company may require that Company-owned metering be installed to monitor the 
customer's generation. The Company reserves the right to inspect the customer's relays and protective 
equipment at all reasonable times. 
Notwithstanding any other provision in this tariff, the following terms and conditions will apply to customers 
that will use a monthly maximum demand equal to or greater than 50,000 kW and requesting service on or

after the effective date of this tariff. 
 
 
1. 
The customer shall enter into a contract with the Company for an initial period of a minimum 
of eight (8) years.  
 
2. 
During the Load Ramp Period, Minimum Demand Charges shall be calculated based on a 
monthly billing demand equal to 60% of the customer’s Load Ramp Contract Capacity. After 
the Load Ramp period, Minimum Demand Charges shall be calculated based on a monthly 
billing demand equal to 60% of the greater of (a) the customer’s Contract Capacity, or (b) 
the customer’s highest previously established monthly billing demand during the past 11 
months.  
3. 
The Company will commit to delivery of Contract Capacity on the schedule designated in 
the contract. In the event the Company is unable to meet the contracted delivery date, the 
Company will notify the customer as soon as practicable and initiate a consultative process 
to develop a remediation plan or to provide temporary power delivery solutions to the 
customer. Remedial actions taken by the Company in response to a delay that are not 
caused by the customer shall not result in additional charges to the customer. Further, in 
the event of delay, the term to which Minimum Demand Charges would apply will be 
reduced commensurate with the delay. Finally, if the Company is unable to meet the 
delivery date in the contract and no remedial actions have been taken to mitigate the 
problem on a temporary basis within one (1) year of the delivery date, the customer will be 
permitted to terminate its Contract or reduce its contract capacity without any fee or other 
penalty. 
 
4. 
The customer, if not having both (a) a credit rating of at least A- from S&P Global Inc. 
(“S&P”) and A3 from Moody’s Corporation (“Moody’s”) and (b) cash and cash equivalents on 
an audited balance sheet prepared in accordance with Generally Accepted Accounting 
Principles (“GAAP”) (“Liquidity”) greater than ten times the collateral requirement, must 
provide a guarantee or collateral at the time of signing the contract equal to fifty (50) percent 
of the total Minimum Demand Charges for the first eight (8) years of the contract, calculated 
based on the Company’s rates in effect that the time the customer satisfies the collateral 
requirement. 
 
 
5. 
The customer may reduce or terminate Contract Capacity subject to a twenty-four (24) 
month notice requirement and a Capacity Reduction/Termination fee that is calculated 
based on the Company’s foregone net revenue requirements from Minimum Demand 
Charges over the remaining contract term after accounting for the release or transfer of the 
terminated capacity to serve new load. 
 
 
6. 
Notwithstanding any provision in this tariff, the Company and its customers will have the 
flexibility to mutually agree to contract terms that vary from the terms in this tariff.

Pilot Distributed Generation Service 
 
Consistent with the terms and conditions adopted in the Opinion and Order in Case No. 23-23-EL-SSO 
this pilot program is extended and modified to be available to secondary and primary voltage customers 
with more than 1MW and less than 7.5MW of on-site renewable energy generation connected to their 
facility and defined as a net metering system under RC 4928.01 (A)(31). Additional participants include 
behind the meter Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS) that are not defined as a net metering system 
under RC 4928.01 (A)(31). The pilot will be limited to an aggregate renewable energy generation and 
BESS capacity of 100MW.

Customer

Secondary 
$8.99/kW 
$9.40 
Primary 
$8.99/kW 
$138.50 
 
For purposes of this pilot, the Excess kVA Demand provision will not apply. 
Monthly Billing Demand 
Monthly billing demand shall be computed for each participation year as the average hourly demand at 
the time of the Company’s six seasonal monthly peaks during the previous calendar year. The same 
monthly billing demand shall be used for each billing month of the participation year, defined as the 
consecutive billing months of July through June. Except for any initial partial participation year for 2021, 
customer participation will be on an annual participation year basis (July to June). Participation will 
continue on an annual basis each participation year unless the customer cancels participation by 
providing notice by June 1 for the next participation year. If the customer chooses to no longer participate, 
by providing notice prior to the end of a participation year, the customer will be returned to service under 
the application General Service Tariff. The Company will provide the customer with the monthly billing 
demand for the next participation year by May 15 of each year. The dates and times of the Company’s six 
seasonal monthly peaks shall be the Monthly Peaks as filed on page 401b of the company’s FERC Form 
No. 1 for the months of January, February, June, July, August and December. 
 
All other rider and charges will be assessed on the General Service Secondary/Primary rates.

*1  The Commission finds:
(1) Eastern Natural Gas Company, Ohio Gas Company, The River Gas Company, The Cincinnati Gas & Electric Company, The
East Ohio Gas Company, Pike Natural Gas Company, West Ohio Gas Company, Dayton Power and Light Company, National
Gas & Oil Corporation, and Columbia Gas of Ohio, Inc. (“Companies”) are gas or natural gas companies as defined by Section
4905.03(A)(5) and (6), Revised Code, and public utilities by reason of Section 4905.02, Revised Code. As such, the Companies
are subject to the jurisdiction of the Public Utilities Commission (“Commission”) in accordance with Sections .4905.04 and
4905.05, Revised Code.
(2) Rule 4901:1-14-08, Administrative Code, Hearings requires the Commission to hold a public hearing at least 30 days after
the filing of each audit report required under Paragraphs (C) and (D) of Rule 4901:1-14-07, Administrative Code.
(3) A public hearing shall be held in these matters pursuant to Section 4905.302(C), Revised Code, to allow the Commission to
review the gas purchasing practices and policies of the Companies and the operation of their purchased gas adjustment clause
and related matters. Each of the hearings shall be held beginning at 10:00 a.m. at the offices of the Commission, 180 East Broad
Street, Columbus, Ohio. At that time, all interested persons will be given an opportunity to be heard. The first day of each
Company's hearing shall be as follows:

(7) Rule 4901:1-14-07, Administrative Code, Audits, also requires an independent auditor and/or consulting firm, selected by
the Commission, to perform a management/performance audit of the Company's compliance with the provisions of Chapter
4901:1-14, Administrative Code. These management/performance audits shall be for the time periods as identified in Paragraph
(6), above, for the following companies: Eastern Natural Gas Company, Ohio Gas Company, The River Gas Company, The
Cincinnati Gas £ Electric Company, The East Ohio Gas Company and Pike Natural Gas Company. The Commission Staff will
oversee these audits. In order to assist the Commission Staff in its oversight, all interviews with company personnel must be
conducted at the Company's Ohio offices. The management/performance audit reports shall identify and evaluate the specific
organizational structure, management policies, procedures, and reasoning of each Company's existing or proposed procurement
strategy, as well as include management recommendations, as described in Rule 4901:1-14-07 (D), Administrative Code.
(8) Pursuant to Rule 4901:1-14-07(D), Administrative Code, the Commission Staff has developed a Request For Proposal
(RFP) entitled, A Management/Performance Audit of the Gas Purchasing Practices and Policies of Regulated Gas Distribution
Companies, to solicit responses for conducting the management/performance audits for each of the companies identified in
Paragraph (7), above.
(9) The Commission has reviewed the RFP and finds that its provisions are consistent with the Commission's objectives in this
matter. The RFP should be distributed immediately to potential proposers. Bach auditor or consultant receiving this RFP will

In the Matter of the Regulation of the Purchased Gas..., 1993 WL 13742105...

have the opportunity to bid on one or more of the management/performance audits. Proposals will be due March 26, 1993. The
selection criteria to be used by the Commission to determine the selection of the management/performance auditor shall be the

by the Company consistent with Rule 4901:1-14-07(A), Administrative Code.
(11) The GCR auditors shall deliver the audit reports to the Commission or its Staff no later than thirty days prior to the scheduled
hearing date.
(12) The GCR auditors shall perform the audits as independent contractors. Any conclusions, results, or recommendations
formulated by the auditors may be examined by any participant to the proceeding for which the audit report was generated.
Further, it shall be understood that the Commission and/or its Staff shall not be liable for any acts committed by the auditors
or their agents in the preparation and presentation of the audit reports.
(13) The GCR auditors will execute their duties pursuant to the Public Utilities Commission's statutory authority to investigate
and acquire records, contracts, reports, and other documentation under Sections 4903.02, 4903.03, 4905.06, 4905.15 and
4905.16, Revised Code. The GCR auditors are subject to the Public Utilities Commission's statutory duty under Section 4901.16,
Revised Code, which states:
Except in his report to the public utilities commission or when called on to testify in any court or proceeding of the public
utilities commission, no employee or agent referred to in Section 4905.13 of the Revised Code shall divulge any information
acquired by him in respect to the transaction, property, or business of any public utility, while acting or claiming to act as such
employee or agent. Whoever violates this section shall be disqualified from acting as agent, or acting in any other capacity
under the appointment or employment of the commission.
(14) Upon request of the auditor or Staff, each Company being audited shall provide any and all documents or information
requested. The Company may conspicuously mark such documents or information “confidential”. In no event, however, shall
the Company refuse or delay providing such documents or information. The Staff or the auditor shall not publicly disclose any
document marked “confidential” by the Company, except upon three days' prior written notice of intent to disclose served upon
the Company's counsel. Three days after such notice, the Staff or auditor may disclose or otherwise make use of such documents
or information for any lawful purpose, unless the Company moves the Commission for a protective order pertaining to such
documents or information within the three day notice period. The three-day notice period will be computed according to Rule
4901-1-07, Administrative Code. Service shall be complete upon mailing or delivery in person.

In the Matter of the Complaint of Gary S. Bohn,..., 1985 WL 1171846...
 © 2026 Thomson Reuters. No claim to original U.S. Government Works.
1
1985 WL 1171846 (Ohio P.U.C.)
In the Matter of the Complaint of Gary S. Bohn, Complainant,
v.
The Cincinnati Gas & Electric Company, Respondent,
Relative to charges for which complainant alleges he is not responsible.
Case No. 85-127-GE-CSS
Ohio Public Utilities Commission
February 20, 1985
ENTRY
By: Colleen L. Mooney, Attorney Examiner
*1  The attorney Examiner, pursuant to the authority granted by Rules 4901-1-14 and 4901-1-27, Ohio Administrative Code,
makes the following findings:
1) On January 30, 1985, a complaint was filed with the Commission by Gary S. Bohn (complainant), Cincinnati, Ohio, against
The Cincinnati Gas & Electric Company (CG&E) alleging that CG&E billed him for service at a former residence at 250 Senator
Place, Cincinnati, Ohio, for two accounts after Mr. Bohn had vacated the residence and after Mr. Bohn and contacted CG&E to
have the accounts taken out of his name. The complaint further alleged that the complainant was not responsible for the charges
after he had vacated the residence and after he had informed CG&E of such and that he had made considerable effort to prove
to CG&E that he was not responsible to pay the disputed bills for the two accounts.
2) CG&E filed its answer on February 14, 1985, which denied the substantive allegations of the complaint. CG&E denied that
the complainant had contacted CG&E to terminate his contract for utility service on three utility accounts. The answer stated
that one final bill was paid in full on July 3, 1980, after a final bill on one account was rendered June 5, 1980. The answer further
alleged that the complainant knew that he had received one final bill and that he did not receive final bills on the remaining
two accounts and that he had a responsibility to inquire about the remaining two final bills. The answer further alleged that the
remaining two accounts were final billed in October 1980, and that prior to October 1980 the bills were not returned by the
mail to CG&E, which would indicate that the bills for the two accounts were either delivered or forwarded to the complainant.
After October 1980, bills on the two accounts were returned, to CG&E as undeliverable. CG&E asked that the complaint be
dismissed on the basis that this Commission has no jurisdiction over controversies between parties as to contract rights and on
the basis that the complainant has failed to meet his burden of proof that CG&E acted unjustly or unreasonably
3) Reasonable grounds for complainant have been stated as required by Section 4905 26, Revised Code, as to the alleged
rendering of an unjust and unreasonable bill. Therefore, CG&E's request for dismissal should be denied. Under 4905 26, Revised
Code, this Commission has jurisdiction over a complaint against any utility by any person that any charge or service rendered
is in any respect unjust or unreasonable. A public hearing should be schedule to commence on April 9, 1985, at 10:00 a.m., at
the offices of the Commission, 180 East Broad Street, Columbus, Ohio 43215.
4) In Commission proceedings, the complainant has the burden of proving the allegations of the complaint. At the hearing it
shall be the complainant's responsibility to appear and he prepared to present evidence in support of the complaint.
*2  The Secretary of the Commission should cause the following notice to be published once not less than 15 nor more than
30 days prior to the hearing in at least one newspaper of general circulation in Hamilton County, Ohio:

2
LEGAL NOTICE
Notice is hereby given that Gary S. Bohn has filed a complaint with the Public Utilities Commission of Ohio against The
Cincinnati Gas. & Electric Company. The complaint alleges that CG&E billed complainant for service on two accounts at his
former residence at 250 Senator Place, Cincinnati, Ohio, after complainant had vacated the residence and informed CG&E
of such. A public hearing on this matter, Case No. 85-127-GE-CSS, has been scheduled to commence on April 9, 1985, at
10:00 a.m., at the offices of the Public Utilities Commission of Ohio, 180 East Broad Street, Columbus, Ohio 43215. Further
information may be obtained by contacting the Commission.
THE PUBLIC UTILITIES COMMISSION OF OHIO By: Mary Ann Orlinski, Secretary
It is, therefore,
ORDERED, That CG&E's request to dismiss the complaint is denied. It is, further,
ORDERED, That the public hearing on this matter be held in accordance with Finding No. 3. It is, further,
ORDERED, That the notice of the hearing be published in accordance with Finding No. 5. It is, further,
ORDERED, That a copy of this Entry be served upon each party of record.
End of Document
